10 Ocarina of Time Remake Changes That Break the Hearts of Nostalgic Fans

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Covering the video games industry since 2017, with experience in news, articles, and reviews. Currently a writer at DualShockers and gaming magazines.

The remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time has brought one of the most beloved and iconic adventures in gaming history back to life with a revamped visual presentation, plenty of gameplay improvements, and a surprising number of small changes that make exploring Hyrule feel fresh all over again. From quality-of-life improvements to the inventory and equipment systems to subtle environmental tweaks, there's literally a lot to appreciate about Nintendo's new take on this timeless classic.

However, as someone who grew up playing the original (and has an admittedly unhealthy amount of nostalgia running through my veins), I couldn't help but notice that some of the little things I loved are no longer around – or they are, but in a very different way.

Thomas Mahler believes Nintendo missed an opportunity to improve the level design in the Zelda: Ocarina of Time Remake.

Don't get me wrong, I absolutely adore this remake and described it as a lovely modern remake with an old soul, but that doesn't mean I'm not allowed to miss a few things from my childhood, right? So, please allow me to put my nostalgic glasses on for a moment and revisit ten changes that have left a tiny hole in my heart.

One of my favorite things about the original Ocarina of Time was starting a new save file and giving Link the most ridiculous name I could think of. It was such a simple feature, but it opened the door to some quite hilarious moments, especially when characters started addressing the legendary hero using whatever silly name I'd come up with.

One of my personal favorites was naming him “Bro.” Suddenly, every conversation in Hyrule sounded like everyone was part of the most laid-back adventure imaginable.

Unfortunately, the remake has decided to put an end to our creative freedom by locking the protagonist's name to Link. I can understand the reasoning, especially considering Nintendo's efforts to make the franchise more accessible to new generations and expand its presence across other media (since the live-action movie is coming hot). Still, I'll always miss my little adventures with Bro. You will be missed, buddy.

One of the coolest easter eggs in the original Ocarina of Time was hidden in the courtyard where you first meet Princess Zelda. As Child Link, you must sneak past the castle guards to reach the princess without getting caught. Once you've had your little chat with her, however, there's nothing stopping you from exploring the area and discovering a few fun secrets.

Among them is a window you can shoot with your Slingshot, prompting a particularly grumpy guard to throw a bomb in your direction (how rude!). But that's not all. If you look through the other windows, you'll find three familiar faces from the Super Mario universe: Mario, Luigi, and Bowser, all sporting their Super Mario 64 appearances.

I was honestly expecting Nintendo to bring this little crossover back using the characters' more modern designs, perhaps even their Super Mario Odyssey models. Instead, the entire reference has been removed from the remake. It's a shame, considering how charming these little Nintendo Easter eggs can be. At least the guard is still there to throw a bomb at your face. Some traditions must be preserved, I suppose.

If there's one thing every Ocarina of Time veteran knows, it's that Rupees are important, and there's no shame in taking advantage of a good farming opportunity. One of the earliest places where you could stock up on some extra cash was a room inside Hyrule Castle's Market, conveniently filled with breakable pots.

As Child Link, you could simply walk inside, smash everything in sight, collect the Rupees, leave, and come back to find the pots replenished. Rinse and repeat until your pockets were sufficiently full.

Unfortunately, the remake has decided that we shouldn't be allowed to get rich quite so easily. While you can still find some pots containing Rupees in the room, they no longer respawn simply by leaving the castle and coming back inside. Instead, you'll have to wait quite a while and spend some time doing other activities before they return. I guess we'll just have to go back to cutting grass like the rest of Hyrule's hardworking citizens.

I know what you're thinking – why on earth would anyone miss Kaepora Gaebora being annoying? But hear me out, I promise there's a reason behind my questionable opinion!

I also ain't a fan of the original owl. In fact, I probably spent more time than I'd like to admit accidentally answering “No” when he asked whether I wanted to hear his explanation again. And if you've played the original, you know exactly how frustrating that could be. However, there was something strangely entertaining about collectively hating this overly talkative bird. Kaepora became one of those characters that practically every Ocarina of Time player had a story about, and that shared frustration was part of the experience.

Now, the remake has given him a much cuter appearance, and he's also significantly less annoying, behaving much more like the wise and mysterious owl he's supposed to be. I mean, where’s the fun in that? I can't help missing the days when simply seeing him fly toward me filled me with dread.

Ocarina of Time has plenty of memorable exploit tricks, but one of my personal favorites involved getting a Heart Piece much earlier than Nintendo originally intended.

In the original game, you could perform a particularly well-timed backflip from the top of Death Mountain, near the entrance to Goron City, and land on the roof of Dodongo's Cavern. This allowed you to grab a Heart Piece that was normally intended for Adult Link, long before you were supposed to have access to it. It was just one of those silly little tricks that made exploring Hyrule feel even more rewarding.

Well, those days are over. The remake has adjusted the backflip's height, making it considerably less effective, and even tweaked the position of Dodongo's Cavern entrance to prevent players from pulling off the same trick (not that I tried to do it or anything).

From suspicious house designs to familiar songs, fans think they've found clues.

There's an unwritten rule among players: the moment you unlock the Hookshot, you must immediately equip it and start shooting at everything in sight. And one of your first destinations will probably be the rooftops of Kakariko Village.

This was really fun because it offered more freedom than simply using it to reach the obvious targets. You could experiment with different surfaces, discover unexpected ways to navigate the environment, and generally mess around with an incredibly useful piece of equipment. Apparently, however, that wasn't quite what Nintendo had in mind. The remake has restricted the Hookshot's functionality, meaning it can now only latch onto specific designated targets, such as stone markers and certain treasure chests.

I understand that this might have been done to prevent unintended shortcuts or make progression more controlled, but I can't help feeling that something has been lost in the process. One of the most enjoyable aspects of Ocarina of Time was experimenting with your equipment and discovering creative ways to use it. So, please, Nintendo, give us our old Hookshot back!

One of my most vivid childhood memories of Ocarina of Time involves something that probably sounds completely ridiculous to anyone who didn't grow up playing the original: stepping into Hyrule Field for the very first time and desperately running toward Hyrule Castle like a lunatic.

That happened because, if night fell before we reached the castle gates, they would close, leaving Link stranded outside until morning – and Hyrule Field at night was not exactly the safest place to be.

And you’ve guessed: the remake has removed this particular inconvenience, meaning you no longer have to worry about being locked out of Hyrule Castle after sunset. My adult self appreciates not having to deal with that little problem, but the child in me misses those frantic nighttime sprints.

There's something wonderfully weird about the little laboratory at Lake Hylia. Inside, you'll find a rather eccentric scientist who spends most of his time complaining about his back pain while standing next to a water tank.

He'll even offer you a reward if you manage to dive deep enough to touch the bottom of the pool. Naturally, being the helpful hero that you are, you'll want to give it a try. But once you reach the bottom, you'll discover something rather unexpected: a shark inside a cage.

What made this little discovery even funnier in the original game was the shark itself! Rather than featuring a detailed 3D model, it was represented by a hilariously low-resolution, static image. It looked so out of place that it became one of those bizarre little details you simply couldn't forget. While the remake has kept the shark in the aquarium, unfortunately, it has also given it a proper visual upgrade. Sure, it looks considerably better now, but I miss the ridiculous charm of that pixelated little creature.

If there's one thing Ocarina of Time does exceptionally well, it's turning a seemingly innocent adventure into a horror game without any warning! One moment you're exploring a dungeon and collecting treasures, and the next you're desperately running for your life from something that looks like it escaped from your worst nightmare.

Few enemies capture that feeling better than the Wallmaster, a giant disembodied hand that suddenly drops from the ceiling, grabs Link, and sends him all the way back to the beginning of the area or dungeon.

And what made this enemy so terrifying in the original was the anticipation. You couldn't simply look up to check whether it was coming, so you had to rely on an increasingly unsettling sound effect and the shadow that gradually appeared above Link. The remake preserves most of these elements, but introduces one rather important difference: a spotlight that illuminates the area whenever it’s about to appear. Combined with its shadow, this makes the enemy practically announce its arrival in the most dramatic way possible.

I appreciate the warning, but I honestly miss the uncertainty that made these encounters so frightening in the original. Thankfully, the Dead Hand is absolutely nightmare-inducing in the remake, so I'll let this one slide.

Remember the Water Temple? If you played the original Ocarina of Time back in the day, there's a good chance that simply reading those two words made you feel a little uncomfortable.

For years, this dungeon had a reputation for being one of the most frustrating experiences in the entire game. Between constantly changing the water level and switching between the Iron Boots and other equipment, it was enough to test even the most patient players.

There was also a particularly nasty Small Key hidden underneath the floor of the central pillar, which could only be accessed after raising the water level. It was incredibly easy to overlook, and if you ever found yourself seemingly stuck with no idea how to progress, there's a good chance this little key was the culprit!

Despite keeping most of the original dungeon's layout intact, the remake has made things considerably easier by removing this easily missed Small Key altogether. This means players no longer have to spend ages retracing their steps and wondering whether they've somehow managed to softlock their game.

Of course, the Water Temple is still a memorable dungeon, and I'm certainly not complaining about a more streamlined experience. But I do find it funny that an entire generation of players had to suffer through those confusing corridors just so newcomers could have a slightly more pleasant time! You're welcome, younger Zelda fans. We fought in the trenches so you wouldn't have to.

Eiji Aonuma and the development team for the Ocarina of Time remake discussed how one of the original's infamous dungeons is being improved.

“Evil All Along”: How Russell Brand Got Caught

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A once-deified celebrity who lured women under the guise of humor has been jailed for 10 years. Here, two sexual assault attorneys and the woman who spearheaded the investigation that started it all weigh in on the work to expose Russell Brand — and what precedent his landmark conviction sets for fellow industry predators: "They should be very scared."

“Whatever women wear, wherever they go, indeed whatever they drink, they retain the right to say ‘no’ and to demand justice from criminal courts when sexual predators like you, Russell Brand, act without their consent.”

Those were the words that rang out across Southwark Crown Court as Justice Joel Bennathan sentenced the once-worshipped presenter, comedian and actor to 10 years and six months in prison on Monday. Hordes of journalists and paparazzi effectively set up camp for four weeks while the jury was taken through extensive allegations of rape and sexual assault. In total, six complainants accused Brand of frantic attacks from 1999 to 2009.

Here, the man who went on to carve out a career for himself in Hollywood films like Get Him to the Greek, Forgetting Sarah Marshall and Rock of Ages — a previously famed womanizer, sell-out stand-up and the ex-husband of pop superstar Katy Perry — was bleakly depicted in court sketches showing the now-devout Christian with his head bowed, rolling a rosary between his fingers.

And last week, after consistently denying any criminal wrongdoing whatsoever, he was found guilty on four of seven counts. Three were frenzied sexual assaults, and one was a violent anal rape that took place after a woman had consensual vaginal sex with Brand at his Hampstead home in 2009. Bennathan’s sentencing remarks capture the harrowing reality of the victim’s experience. He told Brand: “You flipped her over, removed the condom and, without asking, forced your penis into her anus, causing her great pain. She asked you to stop, then asked you again loudly. You replied, and I quote, ‘I want you to feel my cum.’ You ejaculated. She was distraught and in severe pain. The bed was heavily soiled by blood, sperm and faeces. You told her it was her mess, and made her try to clear it up. Shortly after, you went off to get ready, then went to work. She was driven to a nearby underground station by your driver, who for some reason had tissues ready to hand to her as she wept. She was left distraught, in pain and terrified she might have contracted HIV.” The courtroom was completely silent.

Over the last month, this trial has dominated the media, spawned entire podcast shows, and divided the British public. Brand admitted during the trial that no one would “touch him with a bargepole” and that he had failed to persuade anyone to testify on his behalf. Naturally, many of those familiar faces on the British comedy circuit distanced themselves long ago from the sex offender. Jonathan Ross released a statement this week saying he is “horrified” by Brand’s crimes and was “genuinely unaware” of his conduct; there are quite literally hundreds of others who have not — and won’t be — saying anything.

And as Brand now embarks on what looks like a seven-year stay at HMP Wandsworth prison, a number of questions remain unanswered. Is it true that his lurid behavior was “an open secret” across the British TV industry, and why were so many of these allegations about incidents in the workplace? Why had Brand’s defense, led by a barrister named Ian Winter, claimed that journalists “poisoned the well of justice” by publishing various investigations into Brand’s behavior? How, with the sheer brazenness of Brand’s actions palpable in each attack he was found guilty of, did this predator stay undetected for at least 17 years? And what message does his conviction send to others who still operate as he did?

Two sexual assault attorneys, a scholar of work, pay and insecurity in the creative industries at the University of Warwick, as well as the woman who spearheaded investigations at Channel 4 and Sunday Times that would kickstart a police probe into Brand’s crimes, talked to The Hollywood Reporter about how Brand’s conviction has changed the U.K. entertainment industry. They lay bare the specifics of a freelance-heavy industry that allowed Brand to evade punishment, how women were lured into spaces with him under the guise of flamboyant comedy, and the dedicated, years-long work of investigative reporters who helped bring a celebrity rapist to justice.

Dorothy Byrne was head of news and current affairs at Channel 4 when Jemima Goldsmith, Brand’s ex-girlfriend, came to talk to her in 2019. “People would say, ‘How did you get onto the story?’ And I would say, ‘Oh, well, somebody told me about it,'” Byrne begins to THR.

It is only in the last 48 hours that Byrne has been able to publicly name Goldsmith as that whistleblower. The TV producer, who dated Brand for around a year in 2014, took to social media after his sentencing this week to detail her own experience of “violence, coercive control and abuse” at the hands of Brand. “I didn’t know about Russell Brand,” says Byrne. “I thought he was creepy and sleazy — and we now know that massive numbers of people in the media industry knew what a terrible person he was.” (Brand scarcely attempted to hide it himself; one of his stand-up tours in 2006 included an entire section dedicated to Brand telling the audience he likes “blowjobs where mascara runs a little bit.” A former friend testifying for the prosecution said Brand bragged about wanting to sleep with 5,000 women.)

Byrne continues: “But I worked in news and current affairs. Not comedy and entertainment. [Goldsmith] told me of her experience and she said, ‘I think he should be exposed to protect other women.’ Even to have that conversation was, for her, difficult, and I really admire and thank her for it.”

This would be the spark that would ignite a joint investigation between Channel 4’s Dispatches, The Times and Sunday Times, with two reporters — Rosamund Urwin and Charlotte Wace — leading the charge into reports, and eventually interviewing hundreds of people on the topic of Brand’s behavior. Byrne left her Channel 4 role in 2020 and maintains she was only involved in the early investigation. But even then, she says, other national outlets were tracking the story. “[They’d] found it very difficult,” she tells THR. “In this case of Brand, women were particularly afraid to talk. Firstly, because he was just so famous. They felt he was so famous and powerful. He’s got loads of money. ‘If I speak, he’s got all the power.’ And nearly everybody who works in TV now is freelance.”

And so came one of the first major hurdles in this case against Brand — the enduring trickiness of temporary contracts. Dr. Heidi Ashton, an expert in work, pay, and insecurity in the creative industries, says of the freelancer-filled TV industry in which Brand preyed on women: “A freelancer’s future employment depends heavily on maintaining a good reputation with the individuals who hire them, rather than with the organization issuing their contract. Raising concerns about bullying, harassment or unsafe working practices can therefore carry substantial professional risks — who would they go to with these issues? Workers may fear losing not only their current role but also jeopardizing their career.”

Much has been said of the men around Brand who watched the attacks unfold and did nothing. One of the sexual assaults involved Brand grabbing a woman’s breasts in a busy Soho bar in 2004; another saw him pushing a victim up against a wall, kissing her body and squeezing her breasts and buttocks “aggressively” — in the Capital Radio office. Last week, a former associate of Brand’s, Jack Bayles, was accused of watching and laughing while the entertainer placed his head and nose in a co-worker’s “vaginal area,” one of the assaults Brand was found guilty of. It took place as they worked on a Channel 4 project. (Bayles, whose credits include Michaela Coel’s I May Destroy You and Kat Sadler’s Such Brave Girls, has officially been asked to “step back” from his producing role as head of comedy at independent TV production company Various Artists Limited.)

But this complainant did speak up immediately after Brand forced his head into her groin. “It was brushed under the carpet by senior management,” she explained in her victim impact statement on Monday. “This really affected me and had something similar happened again, at the time, I know I would not have had the confidence to speak up again. There was a culture, at the time, which meant I felt I had to just carry on and not talk about it anymore.”

“[Brand] was considered this treasure, and every time he got fired, he’d get promoted to another job. He kept moving around, and I don’t think it’s because he’s got a lot of talent,” says Dr. Ann Olivarius, a leading women’s rights attorney and co-founder of McAllister Olivarius, a law firm working across the U.K., U.S. and Ireland. Brand did indeed find himself bouncing between roles in British TV and radio: in the 2000s, he hosted Big Brother spinoff Big Brother’s Big Mouth and worked on The Big Fat Quiz of the Year, Russell Brand’s Got Issues and Russell Brand’s Ponderland. “His employers didn’t stop it,” Dr. Olivarius continues about his behavior. “It was all considered well and good. What is that old thing men say? ‘If you can get laid, then lucky you.’ I haven’t met a woman who’s told me that yet.”

Byrne, however, believes that the men in these offices were also frightened of how speaking up might affect their careers, with the risk of retaliation higher because of their freelance status. “These contracts are often so short that people’s fear is you just won’t get asked back, and it will get around that you are a complainer or a troublemaker.”

“But there was another thing as well with Brand — women described him as frightening,” she adds. “If you think of some of the evidence in the court … one minute he’s funny and sexy, and then in a moment he changes.”

Women were routinely deceived by Brand’s status as a comedian. “The fact he was a comedian…” Byrne trails off. “This thing of when a man assaults you and he laughs. He’ll laugh and grab you. Brand treated it like a joke.” After Channel 4 began investigating, Byrne quickly became aware of how notorious Brand was among female comedians. “I discovered that female comedians warned each other about him,” she tells THR. His flamboyant nature and sharp wit — Brand himself also admits in his 2007 autobiography My Booky Wook: “I decided to act gay to attract girls” — allowed the shocking violence of his actions to be quickly diluted by that guise of drollery. “When a man laughs and grabs a woman, if the woman then complains, she’s regarded as having no sense of humor… In the world of comedy,” Byrne asks, “what could be worse than being branded as a woman with no sense of humor?”

New York-based sexual harassment and assault attorney Megan Thomas, of Megan Thomas Law, concurs. “I think he did a lot of things in the name of art and in the name of being a comedian and being in show business,” she tells THR. “People could laugh it off as ‘Okay, that’s just part of his persona.’ But it isn’t real. He was really very derogatory towards women, and he had very little respect for them.”

It’s worth noting that Brand is not the first predator to have been brought to justice in the film and TV world, and he certainly won’t be the last. A fellow comic, David Walliams, is the subject of another bombshell investigation from The Guardian and ITV News on Tuesday that alleged he abused his power as a beloved comedy actor and Britain’s Got Talent judge to prey on teenage girls, some as young as 15, for sex. Walliams has vehemently denied he is a sexual predator and said: “I have not broken the law, but I am devastated that some of the women I met 10 to 20 years ago have now expressed hurt or anger towards me.” The Hollywood Reporter has reached out to his reps for comment.

Walliams himself said in that same statement, “a power imbalance is inevitable when one party is famous.” It is a facet of these cases that arises time and time again, which makes women in the entertainment industry particularly vulnerable. “The element of fame really plays a role because when someone is so famous, it is easy to overlook red flags,” Thomas continues. “These women are maybe starstruck to a certain extent. … Everyone around this star is often creating a bubble and just constantly saying good things about them; they think to themselves, ‘No one will ever believe me,’ and this is why we see these cases happen so frequently with celebrities because they get to this godlike state where they think they’re untouchable. And to an extent, they’re not wrong, right?”

Indeed, a couple of complainants in Brand’s case admitted to being fans of his. The anal rape victim met the disgraced star at his comedy gig at the Hammersmith Apollo (after which he saved her number in his phone as “T” for “tart,” according to Justice Bennathan). Not only was Brand able to exploit that sense of awe, but he also had the power — and money, as many of these male celebrities do — to put together a pretty intimidating legal team, who can advise their clients to resort to threats. “We have heard time and time again men using that threat,” says Thomas. “‘Oh, no one will ever hire you. I’ll tell everyone that you’re horrible.’ I don’t know that we’ve heard that in [the Brand] case. I didn’t read that specifically, but we’ve heard that repeatedly in cases involving actors and directors. And even if a woman’s not in show business, she realizes, ‘If I speak out because this person’s a celebrity, my name will get dragged through the mud too.'”

Dr. Olivarius says women fear retaliation in the workplace and can often lose their entire careers. “You lose your job, you can’t get hired, and then [your] whole future is destroyed. So the calculation is: ‘If I just keep quiet about this, and try to put it in a perspective that it’s a bad date as opposed to an actual rape and attack, then I can maybe live with this and I will still have a career,'” she tells THR.

Even though the women testifying against Brand are entitled to lifelong anonymity, living through the trauma every day in court was clearly taxing. Some of them told the jury so themselves, and would fight back tears as they described how Brand forced himself on them.

Winter attempted to argue that these women were spurred on by the 2023 release of Channel 4 and The Sunday Times‘ investigation, in which Brand was accused of several attacks by a slew of women. Winter said Urwin, Wace and the team of journalists working on that documentary had “poisoned the well of justice” by prompting these complainants to reframe their encounters with Brand, decide they were nonconsensual, and press criminal charges. Brand, says Winter, was denied a fair trial because of the media.

Though the jury was ultimately unconvinced by this line of reasoning, Byrne says Winter — who reportedly snarled at Urwin outside the court after Brand’s guilty verdicts came in: “Are you happy now? You f***ed it” — deployed a rhetoric imported by President Donald Trump. “This attempt to say that when journalists do an investigation, it’s criminal… That is new,” she says. “Where does it come from? Donald Trump. This is his playbook. You don’t just attack journalists. You don’t call them liars.” Dr. Olivarius makes a similar point — that the rise of Trump, who himself faces extensive allegations of sexual assault, has led the way, especially in the U.S., for a post-#MeToo dismissal of women’s experiences. “Are women really such liars?” Dr. Olivarius asks. “Can you not trust a woman with her testimony?” She cites the recent Cornell 7 case as an example of that trend.

Putting the other criticisms around Winter’s misogynistic and “shameful” defense aside, both Thomas and Dr. Olivarius maintain that it is extremely rare for women to lie about their rape and sexual assault experiences. “Who wants to brag about sexual interference? Nobody,” says Dr. Olivarius. “I don’t know those women, but there seems to be this image, these rape myths, that somehow women make it up. I’ve never had anybody in my thousands of clients who I felt had made it up.”

These women, now widely commended for their bravery, were indirectly found through the tireless work of investigative reporters at Channel 4 and The Sunday Times. Brand was brought to justice because of that work, no matter how Winter spun it in court. “That investigative journalism helped more victims come forward,” says Thomas.

Brand’s conviction and lengthy sentence have set a precedent — a warning — for fellow industry predators, who are likely still operating. “They should be very scared,” says Byrne. “One of Brand’s offenses was that he reached over and grabbed a woman’s breasts. That has happened every day in TV. Well, it won’t be happening now because he got several months in jail for that.”

Such was the media coverage around the trial — and the livestream of the firm, unemotional, victim-commending statement from Justice Bennathan — that Brand now serves as an illustration of what powerful investigative reporting and courageous victims can do: hold the previously unimpeachable to account. “Several women I know who work in TV said to me before the case, ‘He’ll never be found guilty,'” Byrne admits. “I said, ‘Why not?’ and they said, ‘Because they never get found guilty. They always get away with it.’ But the evidence was there… The coverage was massive, and the public reaction has been huge.”

With the confirmation from the Met Police that Brand is under investigation for two more alleged rapes, Byrne thinks there will be a change. “I think more women are going to come forward. Look at how long he worked in the industry, nearly 30 years, and look at how prolific he was. This man could have hundreds of victims. I think this is a turning point.”

Dr. Olivarius hopes that civil cases will allow the women to receive compensation for what they’ve been through. “With criminal cases, they get no money damages. They get no therapy costs. They get nothing. They just testified. They did the right thing.” She lauds the introduction of Britain’s Employment Rights Act, which introduces sweeping changes across 2026 and 2027 and is set to significantly strengthen rules around workplace harassment, whistleblower protections and non-disclosure agreements. The country remains in dire need of stronger law enforcement and funding for victims’ therapy, she adds as a caveat.

While sexual assault and rape remain “an epidemic” in British, American and Irish society, Dr. Olivarius concedes that Brand’s conviction is unusual. “It’s really rare that they’ve got this guy,” she says. “He was so flamboyant, so bragging, and even in the courtroom, he’s got his Bible, waving it in the air as if it were air freshener. And what this will do to his arrogance and sense of self-satisfaction, I [am] very curious to see.” Thomas believes it proves the existence of a harsher spotlight on male predators in our current climate. “I don’t know if anyone’s listening, but I think we are seeing a second wave of the #MeToo movement,” she tells THR.

And Byrne, who has been privy to Brand’s reign of terror longer than most, is simply just relieved that women across the industry now have proof of what’s possible. “You can do something about it, you can go to the police, you can report them to your employer, and your employer has to do something about it,” she says. She is content that the man now in prison is exactly the man those women said he was. “He’s been left wing, he’s been right wing, he’s been an atheist, he’s been a Christian, he’s been a conspiracy theorist. But he hasn’t been any of those really,” says Byrne. “He’s been evil, criminal Russell Brand, all along.”

Chip In on One Group Gift – Collect Every Share Before Anyone Buys

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Pool your money with coworkers, friends, or siblings for one group gift instead of each buying a small present. Have one person choose it, set a share that fits the tightest budget in the group, and collect everyone’s money through a payment app before buying. Then give it with one card everyone signs.

Six coworkers who each spend $25 on a baby gift hand the new parents six small presents. Pooled, the same $150 buys one registry item that costs six times as much as each small present.

Together, a group can afford a gift none of its members would buy alone, like good luggage for a retiring coworker or a family photo session for grandparents, while each person pays only a share of the price.

A group gift works best when one person runs it and collects every share before buying anything. An organizer who buys first and collects later can spend weeks chasing repayment, or end up covering a share or two.

If you’re organizing, check for a registry or ask someone close to the recipient what they’d like, then set a fixed share or a suggested range, such as $20 to $30. Make it an amount the person with the least to spare can manage, keep what each person gives private, and tell everyone they can sign the card without chipping in.

Collect every share through one payment app by a deadline a few days before you need to shop. If the group doesn’t all use the same app, start a Cash App pool, a fund people pay into through a link you share. Anyone who doesn’t live in New York can chip in with Apple Pay or Google Pay, no Cash App account needed. Everyone in a pool can see what each person paid, so use a pool only with a fixed share.

Then spend what came in. If two of the six coworkers drop out, buy a $100 gift, not a $150 one. Give it with one card everyone signs, and keep the receipt for an exchange or return.

Ask for the money the day you announce the gift, and buy nothing until the deadline passes.

Compare the best money transfer apps to find one the whole group can use.

Zimmermann Hosts a Star-Studded Soirée in Downtown New York City

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Katie Holmes, Uma Thurman, and Naomi Watts came out to celebrate the brand’s cruise 2027 collection.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Inside Indochine’s red-lit, retro-inspired French-Vietnamese dining room in New York City, Zimmermann gathered friends of the brand to celebrate its cruise 2027 collection. Presented on the sun-drenched Côte d’Azur in June, the label’s latest served as the perfect bit of vacation and occasion-ready dressing for November in New York. Now that the lineup has officially hit stores, fashion insiders and fans of the brand alike (including Uma Thurman, Eve Hewson, Katie Holmes, and Naomi Watts) showed up dripping in ruffled gowns, sailor-inspired stripes, chunky shearlings, and ethereal lace and silks from the collection. Against the dark, 1940s-inspired tropical salon interior of Indochine, it was the ideal mix of downtown energy and New York glamour.

As champagne flowed through the bar area, glasses clinked while miniature crab cakes and spring rolls were passed. Zimmermann’s sister duo Nicky and Simone Zimmermann could be spotted catching up with friends new and old. “We have a very, very close connection with New York,” Nicky told ELLE in between greeting guests. “It was pretty much when I started the business, one of the first places internationally that I went to in the ’90s, and it blew my mind. It’s been inspirational to us 100 percent.” It was a rare occasion for the brand; for years, Zimmermann has shown at Paris Fashion Week (after presenting in New York in the mid-2010s) and the duo oscillates between Sydney and Paris for most of the year.

Zimmermann’s painterly and intrinsically bohemian flourishes could be seen in the many outfits of guests, all highlighting the vivid cruise collection. Think: decadent silky draped drop-waisted gowns and paneled lace maxi dresses, or, for the more demure, sleek all-black looks that captured the New York essence. “For me, there’s a little bit of a sense of humor behind it—without insulting anybody—it was about the story when Australia actually beat America in the America’s Cup, which is a very famous sailing race,” Nicky told ELLE of the collection’s inspiration.

“I think it was in 1983, and America had not lost the America’s Cup in something like 173 years; it was a ridiculous winning streak, and Australia came along. The inspiration is more about a David and Goliath kind of story,” she continued. “We came along as the Aussies, not established in the sailing world. They were very cheeky, rogue, kind of Aussie young guys up against this amazing, sophisticated establishment of the New York Yacht Club. And they never lost—and we beat them. So in Australia, it’s a legendary story.” Nicky combined the sportiness of sailing gear with the sort of elite New York Yacht Club preppiness, resulting in colorful silk scarf embellishment or sweaters decked out in accessorized pins. “They would hold the most incredible parties and the women were so glamorous and sophisticated and beautiful and not anything that Australia could relate to at the time, so I loved the contrast.”

Conversation was flowing and blooming anthuriums covered the tables as guests took their seats. Nicky kicked off the dinner with a speech, musing on her early days in New York on the Lower East Side and the many parties she attended in Tribeca before sharing more inspiration behind the collection: “It’s about establishment, and then it’s about people, you know, working really hard, coming together as a team, defeating the undefeatable,” she said, with a laugh.

Next came an intimate performance by the singer and Bad Monkey actress Charlotte Lawrence, who sang two of her original songs, then led a folksy sing-along to “Dreams” by Fleetwood Mac. Even the most guarded of celebs and fashion’s elite couldn’t help but bob their heads and sing a tune. As guests dined on a family-style mix of kale salad, spicy shrimp, grilled salmon, barbecued chicken, and steak, the champagne glass clinks slowed as candles slowly flickered and the evening closed out with lemon tarts.

A little yacht-club polish, a little Aussie irreverence: Zimmermann celebrated cruise 2027 in high style.

Ripple Dominates Monthly Stablecoin Market Growth

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The renowned San Francisco-based blockchain firm associated with XRP, Ripple, has posted substantial growth in its stablecoin market over the last month.

Apart from its substantial growth over the period, the firm has also gained dominance in the sector, outperforming several major stablecoin-issuing firms.

Known for its growth and development, the firm has consistently maintained its dominance in the stablecoin sector. However, the past week saw Ethena challenge Ripple’s dominance by taking over the top spot over a 24-hour period.

Nonetheless, the latest data from Token Terminal shows that Ripple has maintained dominance across the stablecoin market over the last month.

According to the data, Ripple has emerged as the leading contributor to stablecoin market growth over the past 30 days, recording a $258.2 million increase in market capitalization.

This shows that even though Ripple’s associated crypto token XRP has continued to face persistent volatility over the period, Ripple has maintained strong momentum in the stablecoin market.

In addition to its impressive increase over the last month, further data shows that Ripple has outpaced several other major stablecoin issuers over the period.

While Ripple took the top spot in monthly stablecoin market growth, the data shows that Open Standard ranked second, with an increase of $165.1 million over the same period.

While Ethena had frequently ranked next to Ripple, the project took the third spot this time, recording an increase of only $78 million in its market capitalization.

How ‘Godmother of K-Culture’ Miky Lee Earned the Asian Hall of Fame’s Centennial Medal of Lifetime Achievement

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As CJ Group vice chair, Lee helped launch Superstar K, the MAMA Awards and KCON, making K-pop a global phenomenon.

CJ Group Vice Chair Miky Lee has received the Centennial Medal of Lifetime Achievement from the Asian Hall of Fame during an awards ceremony in Los Angeles, which took place on Nov. 5. The honor shows how Lee has steadily built major milestones for both Korean film and K-pop over the years.

The Asian Hall of Fame is a cross-cultural educational and research institution dedicated to recognizing Asian and Indigenous legacy, early-career achievement and interracial collaboration. In a given year, the Centennial Medal of Lifetime Achievement goes to only a handful of people, and recipients are chosen from figures who have built decades of global achievement and influence in fields such as culture, arts and entertainment. That sets the medal apart from the annual Class Inductees, which comprise a larger group of people.

The award carries particular weight because it recognizes Lee’s role over the past 30-odd years as a bridge between Korea and the world. Throughout her career, she has widened the base for the global spread of Korean content and exchange between cultures. Billboard Korea takes a look back at what Lee has done to help build K-pop’s global infrastructure, and at what the Centennial Medal of Lifetime Achievement means.

While Lee’s name is familiar in the film world, she stated during her acceptance speech in Los Angeles on Nov. 5 that music was the starting point. “And me? I simply loved music,” she said, adding that music “has been at the heart of everything we’ve built in culture.” Lee is the figure who created and developed K-pop’s anchor points, building on music platforms that span broadcasting, distribution and live performance.

The origin of the “K-survival audition,” a cradle for K-pop talent

On July 24, 2009, Mnet’s Superstar K first went on air, raising the curtain on Korea’s first modern survival audition show. It went on to produce a long list of standout stars, and countless audition programs followed the show’s model. Lee had been the one to initially suggest that Mnet look into a program of its own after she had taken note of American Idol in the U.S.

In 2016, Mnet’s Produce 101 carried the audition format over into idol survival and set off another craze. The Produce series went on to launch I.O.I, Wanna One and IZ*ONE. Boys Planet series gave rise to ZEROBASEONE and ALPHA DRIVE ONE, and I-LAND2 produced izna.

The stage didn’t stop at Korea. In Japan, the PRODUCE 101 JAPAN series formed JO1, INI and ME:I, and KO1KEYZ debuted through 2026’s PRODUCE 101 JAPAN SHINSEKAI. Today, CJ ENM’s local label business in Japan is broadening its scope beyond audition-linked groups, extending to the development of Japanese boy band DXTEEN.

The “K-audition universe” and “K-star development system” that Lee set in motion are still evolving. Next up is GIRLS PLANET 2027, a global girl group project in partnership with Universal Music Group’s REPUBLIC Collective. The show is targeting a 2027 premiere, and the two sides will collaborate on everything from production to the debut group’s global activities. Superstar K is also set to return next year with its first new season in 11 years, drawing attention from music fans.

From Korea’s first music video awards to the Dolby Theatre: the MAMA AWARDS

The MAMA AWARDS have long been one of the award shows K-pop fans both in Korea and abroad can’t help but look forward to. The show started out as the Mnet Video Music Awards, Korea’s first music video awards. The show combines Mnet’s stage production capabilities with artists’ talents to deliver visuals and performances that are talked about long afterward.

The show’s signature slogan, “Music Makes One,” has become a signal that gets K-pop fans’ hearts racing at the end of each year. Lee invoked the term in her acceptance speech, saying, “‘Music Makes One.’ That was the dream. And the fans brought it to life.” After bringing music fans from around the globe together, the MAMA AWARDS expanded to stages across Asia. In 2024, the show planted its flag for the first time at Hollywood’s Dolby Theatre, the iconic home of the Academy Awards.

Leading KCON as it grew into a global fan community

Lee spearheaded the founding and expansion of KCON, the music and culture festival that launched in California in 2012. KCON was different from the start. Alongside concerts, it offered talk sessions, workshops and artist signing events. Back in 2014 and early on in their career, BTS took the KCON LA stage, with RM later stating in an interview with Mnet that the performance there was what first earned the group a strong response in the U.S.

The road she has traveled, and the road ahead, as K-pop’s new frontier

Alongside pop legend Lionel Richie, Lee was credited as an executive producer of KPOPPED, an eight-part music series released worldwide on Apple TV+ in August 2025. Produced by CJ ENM and Eureka Productions, the show pairs Western and K-pop artists to reinterpret existing hits and perform them in front of a live audience in Seoul. ITZY teamed up with Spice Girls’ Mel B and Emma Bunton, ATEEZ with J Balvin and Kylie Minogue and BLACKSWAN with Boyz II Men for musical reinterpretations on the Seoul stage. The series opened a new horizon for K-pop and moved beyond simply exporting finished products to proving that K-pop artists are shaping the game as “equal creators” alongside the world’s top artists.

The medal was presented to Miky Lee by Lee Soo-man, key producer at A2O Entertainment. When Soo-man was inducted last year, it was Miky Lee who introduced him as the “Godfather of K-pop.” Lee Soo-man opened his remarks by saying, “I have been waiting an entire year for this moment. Tonight, it’s my turn.” His speech showed a relationship that spans more than 50 years.

“If I am the Godfather of K-pop, then Miky Lee is the Godmother of K-culture,” Lee Soo-man said, praising her contributions to culture.

Their speeches doubled as a chronicle of Korean entertainment. Miky Lee looked back on the 1995 investment in DreamWorks and said, “We wanted to help lay the foundation for Korea’s entertainment industry.” She continued, “In the past, nearly 85% of songs on Korean TV were American pop songs, but by the 2000s, it was completely the other way around.” She traced the journey through Mnet, MAMA and KCON, and closed her acceptance by saying, “Now, it’s our turn to believe in the next generation.” The question that now lies is just how far the next generation will carry the K-pop frontier she has expanded.

MotoGP U.S. Rights Head to ESPN in Deal With Liberty Media

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The motorcycle racing circuit, a sister brand top Formula 1, was most recently with Fox Sports.

ESPN is back in business with Liberty Media.

The sports media juggernaut has inked a deal to broadcast MotoGP, the motorcycle racing circuit owned by Liberty, beginning in 2027.

It’s a two-year deal that will cover both the U.S. and Latin America, where ESPN already had MotoGP rights. MotoGP’s most recent U.S. rights were with Fox Sports, and they had previously been with Warner Bros. Discovery and NBC Sports.

ESPN say that the races will air on all of ESPN’s platforms, as well as on Disney+.

Liberty Media, which is led by Derek Chang, owns both MotoGP and Formula 1, which left ESPN this year to move its U.S. rights to Apple TV. Liberty bought MotoGP in 2024 at a $4.5 billion valuation with a goal of building it into a worldwide brand as it has done with F1. The U.S. market, for obvious reasons, is a top priority, so ESPN’s reach is seen as a boost to that effort.

“This agreement with ESPN marks an important milestone in MotoGP’s growth strategy across the Americas,” said Carlos Ezpeleta, deputy CEO of MotoGP. “ESPN’s position as one of the world’s leading sports broadcasters, combined with its strong connection with audiences across the United States and Latin America, makes it the ideal platform for us to continue expanding MotoGP’s reach and bring the sport closer to millions of people. This is an exciting opportunity to introduce MotoGP to new audiences while continuing to serve our passionate existing fan base through one of the most influential sports media brands in the world.”

“ESPN and MotoGP have a strong relationship that dates back nearly 25 years, when we began bringing the championship to fans across Latin America,” added Freddy Rolón, head of global sports & talent office for ESPN. “This is a great example of how relationships that begin in one region of our international business can grow across ESPN’s global portfolio, creating new opportunities to connect different audiences with world-class sporting events.”

Democrats want to stop Trump's arch, golf course and more. What are their options?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President Trump signed the final steel beam at the White House ballroom the day after the midterm elections. Andrew Harnik/Getty Images hide caption

President Trump's efforts to remake Washington, D.C., have dominated his second term, even after his party's resounding midterm defeat.

He started his election week at the scandal-plagued Reflecting Pool and ended it with an order declaring his gilded Oval Office a national monument to "make it impossible for anybody ever to destroy this incredible space."

That proclamation, like any executive order, could be undone by a future president. But many of Trump's construction projects are changing D.C. in more permanent ways.

His administration has already downed hundreds of trees at East Potomac Park and demolished the East Wing of the White House to make space for a ballroom — which he called "the safest place in Washington" as he showed it to reporters the day after the midterms.

Trump has taken a uniquely unilateral approach to construction, packing regulatory commissions with his allies and bypassing Congress entirely. Most of his projects — from the triumphal arch to the Kennedy Center — are now playing out in court.

Critics accuse the Trump administration of skirting federal laws and misleading judges in its effort to progress as quickly as possible before the end of his term.

Democrats have vowed to use their soon-to-be congressional majority to push back on what they describe as Trump's "vanity projects."

When asked about that, White House spokesperson Davis Ingle told NPR in an email that "no other president has done more to beautify our Nation's interior than President Trump." He cited park cleanups, the Reflecting Pool and the White House helipad and ballroom as examples, and said Democrats "should work with President Trump to make our nation's capital beautiful again."

Rep. Jared Huffman, center, poses with activists protesting Trump's triumphal arch at Memorial Circle in May. He will soon become the chair of the committee with legislative and oversight authority over public lands. Andrew Harnik/Getty Imags hide caption

Rep. Jared Huffman, D.-Calif., told NPR in an interview that the Trump administration has "played this game of Catch Me If You Can, where they roll ahead with the bulldozers and the tree cutters … and they wait for courts to shut them down." He says a Democrat-led Congress will fight back.

"There's no way that we could look away while these sacred, iconic places are being permanently changed in ways that violate the law and could have really terrible lasting impacts," said Huffman, the top Democrat on the House Natural Resources Committee, which has a legislative purview over public lands and national parks.

Huffman says blocking Trump's construction projects — and holding people accountable for potential wrongdoing — will be a top priority when he leads the committee. Earlier this week, he sent letters ordering dozens of administration officials and private companies to preserve their records for a future investigation.

"Whether it is the arch or … or the East Potomac golf course or any number of other examples around the United States capital, that is federal land, and we have a direct statutory responsibility to either authorize those changes or to stop them if they're proceeding without the proper authorization," Huffman said.

What would that actually look like? Democrats have many tools at their disposal, from legislation to lawsuits. But experts say only two are likely to succeed: funding and oversight.

Trump made a final pre-Midterm Day pitch to voters from the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool, whose renovations took months longer than anticipated due to algae blooms and peeling paint. Saul Loeb/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

Congress could — at least in theory — pass laws to block specific projects from moving ahead. Some Democratic lawmakers have already introduced bills to protect the Kennedy Center from demolition and prohibit the construction of an arch at Memorial Circle.

But Trump has promised to "veto everything," and Democrats won't have a big enough majority to override him on their own.

"Legislation in principle is the cleanest way of doing it, but it's also not achievable," said David Super, who teaches administrative and constitutional law at Georgetown. "So what they're likely to do is cut off funds."

The Constitution says that the federal government may spend money only if is appropriated by Congress. But Trump has neither sought nor received congressional authorization for projects like the arch and the White House ballroom — he maintains he doesn't need it.

Rep. Chellie Pingree of Maine is one of many who disagree. She's the top Democrat on the House committee that oversees funding for the Interior Department.

"Certainly Congress should have been involved, because we have the power of the purse," Pingree told NPR. "That is our ultimate tool. And Republicans have refused to use that. But we can use that in very many ways to block him or to use it for leverage."

Pingree says a Democratic majority will be able to write appropriations bills with clear language from the outset about where money can and can't be spent — and potentially take the president to court if he doesn't follow it.

Super expects Congress would prevail in such a challenge, because it has clear-cut authority over federal funds.

For example, he noted that some of Trump's D.C.-area beautification projects have been funded at least in part by diverted national park entrance fees. Congress could "cap how much money can be used in the greater Washington area, and force the money to be used in the Everglades and Yosemite and Great Smoky Mountains," he said.

Congress will have to make some appropriations decisions in the coming weeks, since it needs to pass a short-term funding bill by Dec. 11 to avoid a government shutdown. Democrats could use that opportunity to try to limit funding for at least one of Trump's construction projects.

Huffman has proposed language that would prevent Trump from using federal funds for the East Potomac golf course renovations, limiting them instead to repairs of the park's crumbling seawall. Pingree said she will join him in fighting to negotiate that into next month's bill.

On one October Saturday, the Trump administration cut down some 430 trees at East Potomac Park, where the president wants to develop a municipal golf course into a championship-level course. Aaron Schwartz/Getty Images hide caption

"Whether or not the president will sign that with that in there, I don't know yet," she said. "But certainly those are the most … immediate crisis points … People want the president to stop cutting down the trees on the golf course and stop trying to take that over, and they want to make sure that no shovel goes into the ground on building the arch."

Earlier this year Pingree introduced an amendment that would have outright blocked construction of the arch; it failed along party lines. She wonders whether any Republicans might vote differently after the midterms, given the message Americans sent at the polls.

"They want the president to be focused on the issues they're dealing with: the cost of health care, the cost of food, the cost of gasoline," she said. "And if every time the president gives a speech, he's either standing in front of the Reflecting Pool, which he spent millions of dollars on, or showing you the new marble choices he's putting into the ballroom, it just makes a lot of people mad."

Pingree said she's been surprised to hear how strongly people feel about these projects – even people well outside D.C. And she agrees with them that potential corruption and misuse of taxpayer dollars are worth fighting: "After the first of the year, we will use every tool in our toolbox."

Lawmakers and experts acknowledge that not every project is reversible, or potentially even worth the fight. Huffman suggested he will focus on bigger projects, and ones that are moving too fast for courts to adequately rein in.

"Are we going to throw all of our efforts at stopping the gilding of every statue in Washington, D.C.? Probably not," said Huffman. "But these are iconic places that we're talking about, with permanent damage that we need to engage on to try to prevent."

Even so, Democrats could decide that certain construction projects are "the price they pay for other priorities," said Claire Leavitt, a professor of government at Smith College. Voters made clear they want lawmakers to focus on ending the Iran war and making life more affordable, big asks that may take a lot of political capital.

"And that's not corruption or Democrats not doing their job," Leavitt said. "It's just the natural trade-offs of dealing with who you're dealing with, and understanding that what matters to Trump more than anything seems to be building the arch, building the ballroom … remaking the Kennedy Center."

Whether or not these projects move forward, Congress can provide a fuller public accounting of how they came to be — in the hopes of preventing more in the future.

"They can gavel in hearings. They have agenda-setting power," Leavitt said. "They determine what the public hearing roster is. They decide which witnesses to invite. And that means they have a unique opportunity to draw negative public attention to the Trump administration."

Work on the White House ballroom is moving quickly. Andrew Harnik/Getty Images hide caption

That may be easier said than done, especially with an administration that has pointedly refused to comply with congressional oversight and is now warning Democrats that their future investigations could constitute criminality. Huffman was coy when asked how he is preparing to handle such challenges.

"We are going to have to have a strategy and a plan to do these things that prior Congresses have never imagined they would have to do," he said.

One investigative priority, lawmakers say, will be following the money — especially when it comes to donations for the ballroom and other projects. Leavitt says that focus has an added benefit: Private companies and individuals are likely more willing to testify than a member of the administration.

Another example: Congress could subpoena for information about the decision-making processes of the Kennedy Center board and the physical state of the building, which trustees say must be closed for two years of repairs. And it can put that information in the Congressional — and therefore historical — Record.

"Both the current public can make its judgments, and history can make its judgments," Leavitt said.

Leavitt said oversight can often lead to policy change in the longer term. For example: The committee investigating the Jan. 6 insurrection didn't stop Trump from eventually pardoning Capitol rioters, but it did lead Congress to pass an electoral reform act.

"The frustrating part about congressional oversight is Congress can draw attention to a problem, and they can demand accountability for something that's already been done," Leavitt said. "But it's very hard for Congress to stop something from happening that the president is determined to do."

Super agrees that Democrats are playing a long game. He expects to see more bipartisan support after Trump leaves office for preventing future presidents from following his playbook.

"The process in the past has been built on trust, and President Trump certainly broke that trust in numerous ways," he said. "Laws can be written so that we don't depend on trust, we depend on law. And I'm guessing we're going to do that."

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

GTA 6 Nearly Hit With Ban Over ‘Frequent Male Genital Exposure’

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

With the launch of Grand Theft Auto 6 mere days away, we already know to expect a whole lot of mature subject matter. Official promotional materials like the Netflix Extended Look have prominently featured strip clubs, drug deals gone wrong, and chaotic vehicular shootouts. A new report out of South Korea from Inven Global claims members of the Game Content Rating Board (GCRB) nearly refused to issue a rating for GTA 6, which would have effectively banned the game there. The main sticking point? An excessive amount of "out of context genital nudity."

According to the report, the GCRB held three separate review sessions to determine its rating for GTA 6. The first review took place on Sept. 10, before committee members had access to a playable build. That ended in a tie, with three voting in favor of refusal and three in favor of an Adults Only rating. "I do not believe genital exposure to this extent falls within acceptable limits under the current standard," said one of the members who refused a rating. It's possible that this assessment was shaped by the massive GTA 6 gameplay leaks that took place over the summer, which showed Jason interacting with unclothed patrons at a nudist resort and nonchalantly cruising around the streets of Miami with his dong hanging out.

The GCRB met again on Oct. 2, but ultimately opted to postpone its decision a second time, having obtained a copy of the game just a few days earlier. "Further inspection is necessary, but if a rating is actually granted, we should take this opportunity to review changing the regulation that treats simple genital exposure as grounds for refusal," said one of the committee members.

During the final review session on Oct. 16, the committee voted eight to one in favor of issuing an Adults Only (19+) rating. "It appears that they intentionally covered genital exposure in explicitly sexual scenes, while allowing exposure in background settings," explained one voter from the majority. The lone dissenter expressed concerns that this would mean open season on background penises in future games. "Suppose we consider genre characteristics to permit background genital exposure in adventures or RPGs while remaining conservative only with suggestive games," they said. "I am concerned that background genital exposure will frequently appear in future RPGs as a result." Don't get any ideas, Square Enix!

While it is pretty funny to imagine people having high-stakes debates about how much dong is too much dong for a video game, this conflict indirectly tells us some interesting things about GTA 6. We know there will "explicitly sexual scenes" in the game, but you won't see anyone's business district during those moments. In other words, Rockstar isn't bringing the infamous Hot Coffee sex minigame from GTA: San Andreas into 2026. That's probably for the best.

GTA 6 has received strict adult age classifications in virtually every market. It's got an M (17+) rating in North America, and 18+ ratings in Japan, Europe, the UK, Australia, New Zealand, Hong Kong, India, Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, South Africa, Taiwan, Thailand, and Brazil. The game has a 21+ rating in Saudi Arabia and the UAE. GTA 6 will be released on Nov. 19 for PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X.

XRP Funding Rate Hits One-Year High, ETFs Still Bleed

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Home » Ripple (XRP) » XRP Funding Rate Hits One-Year High, ETFs Still Bleed

XRP’s hurdles on the ETF markets conflict with the generally-bullish sentiment on Perpetuals. Who’s right?

XRP’s Derivatives crypto traders have been on the look-out for a bear trap. The latest Binance Funding Rate data showcases a scenario where the 30-day cumulative rate reached 0.18%, the biggest point in more than a year.

CryptoQuant’s analyst Dark Frost has dug into the historical data behind the shift, witnessing a similar structure just two months before XRP hit $3.55 back in 2025. The rolling monthly figure is particularly important to swing traders looking for the price bottom, below all 4 moving averages.

Traders have taken a keen interest in the $0.98 – $1 liquidity pocket, the same that served XRP as a trampoline to $1.67 back in August, 2026. With the positioning on Binance’s Derivatives now on the bullish side, the sentiment needs confirmation from whales buying on Spot.

In regards to the XRP coin funding rate hike on Binance, that kind of shift often precedes a price trend reversal. “Extreme readings in either direction can signal that traders are crowding into the same side of the market”, says Dark Frost on X. The 30-day sum hints at a few scenarios.

🗞️ XRP funding rates on Binance hit a 1Y high while spot ETFs see $10.4M in net outflowsFunding rates for ripple:native perpetual futures on Binance have reached their highest level in more than a year. The last time rates were this high was in October 2025, a few months after… pic.twitter.com/ZZte6Vfud4

Over the period shown, peaks in bullish sentiment coincided with XRP price tops, while the most negative funding readings occurred near market bottoms. XRP has already entered a 30% price correction from the cycle peak, similarly to when XRP rebounded 53% after falling to $0.70.

For CryptoQuant’s experts, this resembles strong conviction. “XRP’s 30% correction illustrates the risk of maintaining a bullish bias as prices fall” said the analyst. However, that comes with a price on its own. The data from Futures markets tells a story of XRP bulls getting beat down.

The liquidation data has been lob-sided, with XRP’s bulls incurring a $11.01 million deficit in the 24-hour time-frame, says CoinGlass. The ratios from all five top-traded XRP coin venues are displaying long versus short figures drastically below 1, meaning a bearish crowd sentiment.

On Wall Street, the bearish sentiment is felt even harder. Spot XRP ETFs recorded one of their worst sessions of the year, with daily net outflows hitting 9.37 million XRP, equal to $10.4 million. The altcoin’s ETF items slimmed by $18 million in two days, leaving bears hungry for more.

‘Ebenezer’ Review: Johnny Depp Blissfully Goes All Scrooge In Ti West’s New Horror-Flavored Take On Dickens Christmas Classic

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

There have been countless iterations on the Charles Dickens yuletide classic, “A Christmas Carol” which was first birthed to the world as a novella in 1843, but has been a favorite of Hollywood for nearly a century. Actors have warmed to the bah humbug of it all in various takes beginning with Reginald Owen in 1938’s A Christmas Carol to perhaps still the best, Alastair Sim in the 1951 version. George C. Scott and Patrick Stewart did well-regarded TV movies. Bill Murray did a contemporary comedic version with Scrooged. Albert Finney turned it all into a big screen musical in 1970. Even Kermit and Miss Piggy jumped on board with The Muppet Christmas Carol, and no less than Mr. Magoo in the evergreen (and personal favorite) Mr. Magoo’s Christmas Carol. In 2017 Christopher Plummer even took a whirl with Scrooge in the clever and wonderful biopic, The Man Who Invented Christmas which was all about Dickens writing the time honored tale. Related Stories News Daisy Ridley, Darren Criss, Travis Fimmel & Judy Greer Join Catherine Zeta-Jones In Tate Taylor's Marriage Counselor Thriller 'Cupid'; Filming Underway -- AFM News Johnny Depp & Ti West Land In A Chilly London For 'Ebenezer' Global Premiere

It seems inevitable though that modern day master of all disguises, Johnny Depp would get a turn at bat, and in the latest version, Ebenezer, he has done just that. To say it is a perfect fit for Depp would be an understatement as he finds his own unique way into the role, but in a bit of a twist manages to unearth more humanity in Ebenezer Scrooge than previous versions that saved all that for the inevitable happy ending. Here, insufferable as he can be, this Scrooge allows us to have more empathy for the man as his own past is revealed as the source of his discontent. The other wrinkle with this version is brought by its director, Ti West, previously known as a horrormeister with the trilogy of X, Pearl, and MaXXXine, but it turns out he is a lifelong fan of the Dickens tale and found it ripe for more emphasis on its horror aspects built in with the infamous ghosts so crucial to the story. After all, the original title of the novella was “A Christmas Carol. In Prose. Being A Ghost Story At Christmas”. Paramount’s campaign for the movie also leans into this with the ad line: “If You Think You Know His Story, Think Again”. Fear not though, we do know the story and this is still all very PG13 and not as bloodletting as the filmmaker’s past filmography. West , and his screenwriter Nathaniel Halpern, have too much reverance for what Dickens hath wrought to mess around with the bones of the tale we know all too well. Paramount’s marketing department wants us to believe this is all new but it really isn’t for the most part, just an excuse for Depp to play in the sandbox. Watch on Deadline

Ebenezer, with fine period production design by Eve Stewart and darkly lit cinematography from Checco Varese, perfectly sets the tone as we meet Ebenezer Scrooge and his even more greedy business partner Jacob Marley (an ideal Ian McKellen), just before Marley succumbs to a heart attack. It is Christmas Eve and neither owner was all too swell to loyal but beaten-down employee Bob Cratchit (an effective Rupert Grint) who is anxious to spend time with his family including wife Clara (Daisy Ridley) and young son Tiny Tim (Freddie Lee Grey). There is also a visit from Christmas-loving nephew Fred (Sam Claflin) who can’t understand his uncle’s disdain for the holiday and what it really means.

As Scrooge retires for the night, the fireworks begin, first with a view from down under from the late not-so-great Marley with a foreboding warning. Then we have a visit from the Ghost Of Christmas Past (a spirited Andrea Riseborough) who takes him back (Arthur Conti is the younger Ebenezer) to his roots growing up, to his own family life, and most notably to his one and only true love Belle (Ellie Bamber) in a relationship doomed to failure as his early inclination for money over love seems to take hold. Here we see the human being, flawed but recognizable, taking his first wrong turn. Cue the Ghost Of Christmas Present (a dynamic turn from Severance Emmy winner Tramell Tillman) who really turns on the gas to show Scrooge a miserable world, including for the Cratchit clan involving death and despair. West finds his deepest dive into the tropes of horror with this sequence, about as dark as any version of A Christmas Carol can get. And then it gets even darker with the Ghost Of Christmas Future (creature actor Robert Strange) who is so imposing he needs to say nothing, just show what will become of Ebenezer – and it is not pretty.

It is no spoiler to reveal how it all works out and Depp clearly is having a blast playing it to the hilt. Fortunately the audience will walk out with their Christmas spirit (if not the spirits) intact.

I am not sure the world needed yet another dance with Dickens and Scrooge. As mentioned at the top of this review there is plenty to savor from the cinema of Christmases Past that hold up quite nicely to revisit year after year after year, but I have to say it is satisfying to see Depp give it a go. After a very rough patch in his life and career, just like Ebenezer Scrooge, this gifted actor is seeing the light again.

Title: EbenezerDistributor: ParamountRelease Date: November 13, 2026Director: Ti WestScreenwriter: Nathaniel HalpernCast: Johnny Depp, Rupert Grint, Daisy Ridley, Andrea Riseborough, Sam Claflin, Ian McKellen, Tramell Tillman, Robert Strange, Ellie Bamber, Arthur Conti, Freddie Lee GreyRating: PG13Running Time: 1 hr 56 mins

'Yes': SBI CEO Yoshitaka Kitao Answers XRP Holder Doubts in One Word

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Yoshitaka Kitao, head of Japanese financial group SBI Holdings, answered "YES" when asked point-blank whether he still trusts XRP.

The question came from an X user. The user wrote that the past few weeks had been "truly exhausting" for XRP holders, and said that token holders feel that Ripple has left them behind.

Kitao answered in English. Most of his recent posts on X are in Japanese, and the rest of them are reposts of other accounts.

Kitao previously worked as an executive at Nomura Securities and later served as CFO of SoftBank. In 1999, he founded SoftBank Investment, which later became SBI Holdings. Today, SBI includes banks, brokerage, and insurance businesses, as well as crypto services.

The partnership began in 2016. SBI joined Ripple's Series B round and launched a joint venture with Ripple, SBI Ripple Asia. SBI owns about 60% of the venture, and Ripple owns the remaining 40%.

The same year, SBI Ripple Asia set up a consortium of Japanese banks to test Ripple's technology for payments, and the consortium later grew to more than 60 banks.

In 2018, SBI launched the interbank app MoneyTap. The same year, SBI's crypto exchange, now called SBI VC Trade, started operations with XRP trading. In 2019, Kitao joined Ripple's board of directors.

From 2020, SBI began handing the token out to shareholders as bonuses, and the payment service SBI Remit launched remittances using it with on-demand liquidity (ODL), including in the major Japan-Philippines corridor.

SBI kept working with Ripple during the SEC lawsuit against the company, which lasted from December 2020 until 2025.

Back on February 15, 2026, in rare long-form tweets in English, Kitao denied rumors that SBI holds $10 billion worth of XRP: "Not $10 billion in XRP, but around 9% of Ripple Labs. So our hidden asset could be much bigger." By his account, the combined valuation of Ripple Labs and its ecosystem is "enormous," and SBI owns more than 9% of all of it.

Kitao also talked about XRP at SBI's quarterly briefing on November 2, 2026. He said that investors buy Bitcoin mostly to hold it, and that the value of XRP depends on its use in payments and business ecosystems.

In August 2025, SBI VC Trade and Ripple agreed to bring the RLUSD stablecoin to Japan. In October 2025, SBI also invested $200 million in Evernorth, a company that holds XRP in its treasury. By now, SBI VC Trade has more than two million accounts.
