CJ McCollum joins NBA on Prime crew, discusses role on Hawks, Damian Lillard's comeback

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

CJ McCollum hit the game-winning shot in the Atlanta Hawks' Game 3 victory over the New York Knicks in the first round of last year's playoffs.

Not only does he remember that shot, but he remembers enjoying being a guest on Prime Video's "NBA Nightcap" postgame show where he appeared in his pajamas and the hosts joked he put the Knicks "to sleep." They all laughed about his role of villain as his son popped in to say hello.

The party doesn't have to stop there as NBA on Prime announced on Thursday, Nov. 12 that the 14-year veteran guard will be a player commentator for several programs this season starting with "NBA Nightcap" on Friday, Nov. 13.

"The conversation and team analysis that night was really fun," he told USA TODAY Sports of the postgame memories, "and I thought the chemistry was there, and I felt like it made a lot of sense (for me to join the Prime team). And I have a great appreciation for what NBA on Prime is doing, what the crew is doing, and how they educate and share authentic analysis."

McCollum, 35, majored in journalism at Lehigh University and has experience hosting his own podcast with "The CJ McCollum Show" and "Pull Up with CJ McCollum."

Among Prime's All-Star team of talent that he is joining is a mix of longtime broadcasters, including Kevin Harlan, and basketball greats like Udonis Haslem, Dirk Nowitzki, Steve Nash and Candace Parker. McCollum said the team is "a really cool atmosphere, really great environment of people that I respect and admire."

"They're very honest. They watch the game. They're students of the game. They're people who appreciate the game and they do a great job of describing why certain things are happening," he continued. "... When you have that group of professionals who have played the game, have talked the game, have lived the game, have been around it and seen it for so long, you get various impactful moments where viewers are able to really get a glimpse of what's actually happening, why it's happening, in an insightful way that they can digest."

McCollum appreciates the craft of journalism and how showing the human element of any story brings people together. As journalism continues to face industry challenges, the 2016 Most Improved Player has hope.

"Storytelling is vital. I think it makes the world a better place," he said. "Being able to hear people's stories, being able to see people's journeys not just in the sports world but in life, I think is something that we all can appreciate.

"And journalism isn't dying. It's just evolving. But I think the people who move with integrity, who do things the right way and really appreciate the craft are continuing to thrive for sure."

McCollum shared with USA TODAY Sports some thoughts about various NBA topics as a sneak peek of what fans can expect from him as a guest commentator on Prime Video.

McCollum re-signed with the Hawks this summer after joining the team from the Washington Wizards last season in the Trae Young trade. He remains a veteran leader even as he is coming off the bench and reached the 17,000 points mark when he scored 21 in Atlanta's loss on Monday, Nov. 9 to the Los Angeles Lakers.

"It means a lot," he said of reaching the scoring mark. "It's a sign of longevity, being able to be around for a long time and play at a high level. It's a sign of hard work and appreciation for the sport. But it's also a sign of playing with good teammates, good coaching staff, people that have been able to help me get the best out of my talent and utilize a lot of the resources that we have around us and turn that into some success on the court."

Jalen Johnson is a rising star for Atlanta who helped the Hawks get to a four-game winning streak before the loss. On Saturday, Nov. 7, the 24-year-old scored 30 points in his return from an ankle injury to help with a comeback win over the Brooklyn Nets.

"Obviously, the scoring speaks for itself," McCollum said. "His vision, I think, could be one of his best traits. He's a great passer, leading transition, elite on the break, great paint finisher, and a guy who rebounds at a high level as well. And I think he's a guy that really appreciates the game, is a student of the game, studies it, lives it, and I think the sky is the limit for him."

Atlanta was on full display last month when the Atlanta Dream made it to the WNBA Finals. Angel Reese and company lost the championship to the Golden State Valkyries, but made headlines with performances from Quavo, Big Boi and a heartwarming national anthem from T.I.'s daughter, Heiress Harris.

McCollum pointed out that, at the time of the interview, the Usher and Chris Brown tour was in town and the Atlanta Falcons were in the middle of the NFL season as extra reason that the city was the place to be.

"The energy is always high in Atlanta," he said. "... Atlanta's in a really great place. It's obviously a place of its own, where you have food, you have culture, you have people from different walks of life."

Something this season that NBA fans were anticipating was the return of Damian Lillard. McCollum was teammates with the nine-time All-Star on the Portland Trail Blazers, where they both started their careers. McCollum was there for eight seasons before being traded to the New Orleans Pelicans midway though the 2021-22 season.

Lillard tore his Achilles tendon in the 2025 playoffs with the Milwaukee Bucks and missed the entirety of last season after going back to Portland. In his comeback this fall, he's averaging 20 points per game, had two 25-point efforts and his jumpshot still looks like water.

"I think there was uncertainty from the outside. But for people that knew Dame, know his work ethic, his diligence, his approach to things, we knew he was going to have this type of comeback," he said. "He's worked extremely hard, and I'm thankful and happy to be his friend and have played with him for so long. And to see him be able to overcome something like this is not surprising at all.

"I've seen how he's worked. (We've) been in contact with each other consistently. That's my guy, spent some time together in Oregon when I was out there in the summer, and this is a guy who takes his craft very seriously. He's a pro's pro, takes care of his body, takes care of his mind, and he's in a great space being home again and being back in Oregon, where it all started, that I think is a match made in heaven for him, and I'm happy to see him thriving."

Another hot topic of conversation in the NBA is the second apron, which was established in the 2023 collective bargaining agreement when McCollum was president of the NBPA. The rule creates what can be compared to a hard salary cap that has firm penalties if exceeded.

"The conception of this years ago was more about creating parity," McCollum said, "owners, teams, the union coming to an agreement that there needed to be some changes within the systems of how to strengthen some of the lower tier markets."

The second apron has been criticized for doing too much to squash superteams and breaking up championship rosters. Bruce Brown specifically said that he had trouble in free agency and teams were using the second apron as a reason not to sign him. Karl-Anthony Towns, who won the NBA Finals with the Knicks last season, also said he's had difficulties getting a contract he thinks he's worthy of.

McCollum said that only three years into the new CBA, it's too early to determine if a rule like the second apron is working.

"When you're creating something, you don't necessarily know how it's going to turn out, obviously," he continued. "And as you kind of go through something, you kind of figure out the amendments that need to take place, make sure that teams aren't being punished for drafting well.

"... We're only a few years into it. There's more things that need to be evaluated. It's hard to project what types of impacts something like an apron is going to have on the actual management and construction of a roster and how that affects contracts in the long run. But I think you need more data, more information to truly judge whether or not you're heading in the right direction."

Houston Rockets guard Fred VanVleet became NBPA president in 2025 and McCollum applauded his successor's handling of the role.

"He's doing a phenomenal job," he said. "A guy who's very mature, has continued to figure out ways to help build the business of basketball, collaborating with the NBA from a partnership standpoint. I think he's doing an excellent job. I think his leadership, obviously, on the basketball court, but his leadership off the court is something that people respect, his peers respect, and excited to see what he continues to do for not only our players but also for the league."

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: CJ McCollum joins NBA on Prime crew, discusses role on Hawks, Damian Lillard's comeback

9 foods in the Mediterranean diet and the trades behind them, per Mayo Clinic

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Mayo Clinic's Mediterranean diet advice works as a set of trades. See what 9 foods replace and how firm the evidence behind each one is.

Most diets are known by what they forbid. The Mediterranean diet makes more sense through what people who follow it eat at almost every meal and what they save for a few nights a week. Produce, whole grains and olive oil are everyday foods in this way of eating, while fish and eggs are among the foods kept for only a few meals each week. How often a food appears is a sign of how much it matters to the pattern, and the pattern sets proportions across a week rather than a fixed menu. That schedule turns most food choices into trades. When a reader moves one food into a daily slot, another food has to leave the plate, and the health case for the change often depends on which food left.

The diet earned its reputation for heart health from people who ate this way as a whole culture, not from people who added one food to an otherwise unchanged diet. The research behind it comes mostly from Mediterranean populations, according to StatPearls. That evidence describes a whole way of eating more than a set of portions. For that reason, health advice on the diet is about starting a pattern of eating, not taking a dose of any single food, and the benefit belongs to the whole pattern before it belongs to any one item. A reader can turn that into a working rule: follow the shape of the diet faithfully, and treat any single serving number as a target to aim near rather than a line that guarantees a result.

Mayo Clinic lays out practical steps for starting a heart-healthy Mediterranean eating pattern, covering foods from seafood and nuts to eggs and herbs and spices. Each of those steps comes down to a trade, and the evidence behind the trades is not equally strong. Some swaps have direct proof behind them. Others are cautious limits set where scientists still disagree. A reader who knows which is which can decide where to be strict and where to be flexible. The slides that follow take each food in turn and look at what it replaces, how strong its case is and how closely a reader needs to follow the amount attached to it.

The vegetable target looks very different on the plate depending on how the vegetables are prepared. One serving of leafy greens is two cups, while one serving of cooked vegetables is half a cup. The same four servings can be a heaped salad bowl or a modest side dish. A reader who struggles to finish large salads can reach the target with smaller portions of cooked vegetables.

Eating produce every day is common ground, but the exact counts are less fixed than the habit. Sharon F. Daley of Cape Cod Hospital in Hyannis, Mass., and Melissa R. Hinson of Conemaugh Memorial describe a guideline framework in StatPearls that differs from Mayo Clinic's daily serving counts for vegetables and fruit. A reader can hold firm to eating produce every day and to fruit in place of dessert, and let the balance between fruit and vegetables follow taste, the season and the budget.

Whole grains belong on the plate every day because their benefit is broad rather than dramatic. Mayo Clinic advises eating them each day, and the research points the same way. A European Heart Journal analysis of over 6,500 adults links whole grains to possible improvement in a group of heart risk factors rather than in just one, according to Harvard Health. Small gains across several risk factors can add up to more than a large change in one. People get that kind of modest benefit only by repeating the habit, so whole grains fit an everyday meal better than an occasional one.

The case for whole grains has limits. The research tracks risk factors, not heart attacks or strokes. It supports whole grains as a sensible staple without showing that they prevent heart disease, which makes them a well-founded bet rather than a settled cure. Mayo Clinic measures whole grains by the ounce, and 1 ounce is about one slice of bread. A single slice of whole-wheat toast at breakfast therefore counts as only one serving. A reader who stops there keeps the daily habit in name more than on the plate.

Eating more than a slice or two is easier when bread is not the only source. Mayo Clinic also names bulgur, barley and farro, which a cook can use as the base of a salad, a grain bowl or a side dish. With those grains on hand, a large daily helping becomes part of an ordinary meal rather than a chore.

Make whole grains the base of the plate at every meal, and count each slice of bread as just one ounce toward the day.

Cooks get the heart benefit of olive oil only when they use it in place of butter. The oil has a reputation as a health food in its own right, but Mayo Clinic includes unsaturated plant fats in the diet because people who use them instead of butter-type fats cut their heart disease risk and their LDL cholesterol. The gain depends on the fat that leaves the pan more than on the oil that goes in. A cook who adds olive oil to salads but still cooks with butter has added a food without making the trade.

The proven payoff of the swap is narrow, and it centers on bad cholesterol. Fats from plants that are unsaturated appear to bring down a person's LDL cholesterol along with total cholesterol. Replacing butter with plant oils and spreads had no effect on HDL cholesterol or triglycerides, according to USDA NESR. The improvement is real, but it touches one part of the blood lipid picture rather than all of it.

The heart-disease benefit rests on softer ground. "Replacing saturated fats with unsaturated fats may help lower the risk of heart disease," Mayo Clinic staff wrote. Research on trading butter for plant oil does not settle the question, USDA NESR reports, and the studies differ on whether the swap raises or lowers cardiovascular risk and by how much. People can count on the swap to improve their cholesterol and should treat any heart protection beyond that as a likely bonus rather than a promise.

Some plant fats belong on the butter side of the trade. Blood pressure stayed the same when palm oil, coconut oil or cocoa butter gave way to unsaturated oils, according to USDA NESR. These tropical fats still count among the ones to swap out, though a reader should not expect lower blood pressure from the change. At the store, the useful test is whether a fat is unsaturated, as olive oil is, not whether it comes from a plant.

Make liquid plant oil the everyday cooking fat in place of butter, and count it as a cholesterol step rather than a cure for heart disease.

Fish is one of the proteins the diet saves for a few nights a week, and part of its value lies in the meat it replaces on those nights. The health case for the fish itself is firmer for some benefits than for others. Mayo Clinic says the omega-3 fats in seafood may curb inflammation and stroke risk, and the NIH Office of Dietary Supplements reports that intake of EPA and DHA, two omega-3 fats, lowers triglyceride levels. The triglyceride effect is stated as established, while the stroke and inflammation benefits that justify eating fish are worded only as possibilities. Mayo Clinic links the omega-3 fats to fatty fish, yet no recommended amounts have been established for EPA and DHA. Omega-3 is a plausible reason to eat fish, not a measured prescription, so the safer bet is the fish itself, eaten as a meal.

Mayo Clinic asks for more fish than the heart association does, setting a target of 2 to 3 seafood meals a week. The American Heart Association calls for 1 to 2 servings weekly, according to the NIH Office of Dietary Supplements. Either number makes sense only as a swap. A fish dinner helps when it takes the place of a meat dinner, not when someone adds it to an otherwise unchanged week. Each fish night also calls for only a modest portion, since an adult serving is 3 to 5 ounces. A reader who reliably eats fish instead of meat at the heart association's lower level is already making the trade, and Mayo Clinic's range is the level to aim toward.

Fish oil does not stand in for fish. The diet ties the benefit to fatty fish. Unless a person's cardiovascular risk is high, the American Heart Association doesn't advise taking omega-3 pills, according to the NIH Office of Dietary Supplements. A person who takes a capsule still eats the meat dinner, so the main part of the trade never happens. The cooking method matters too. "Stay away from deep-fried fish," Mayo Clinic staff said. Frying a fillet turns a fish night back into the kind of meal the pattern is meant to replace.

With seafood, the number is negotiable but the swap is not: grilled or baked fish on a night that would have been meat is the habit that counts.

Nuts belong in the Mediterranean diet as a backup source of fat, not the main one. Extra virgin olive oil does most of the work of supplying unsaturated fat at every meal, and nuts rank behind it as a supplier of that same fat. That supporting role keeps the portion small. The diet calls for nuts in four quarter-cup servings a week, according to Mayo Clinic. A quarter cup is a small handful, so each serving adds a modest amount to the fat a cook already gets from the oil in the pan. A reader who sprinkles almonds on a salad or eats a handful of walnuts in the afternoon is adding to the fat the oil already supplies, not building meals around nuts. Nuts matter here for the healthy fat they contribute to the whole pattern, more than for any food they replace.

The advice also covers a specific kind of nut, not every product that contains them. Only raw, unsalted nuts count toward the weekly target, so a can of salted cashews or a seasoned snack mix doesn't meet it as written. That gives the reader a clear way to divide the advice. The weekly count works as a target to aim near, much like the other serving numbers in the diet. The choice at the store is a firm requirement, and it is the one part of this advice that the reader controls completely every time they shop.

Nuts are the diet's measured extra: a small weekly portion that counts only when it is bought plain.

Mayo Clinic advises eating dairy only in moderate portions and choosing low-fat versions over higher-fat ones. The trade therefore happens inside the dairy case, with leaner products going in and richer ones coming out. For most people, the change would show up at breakfast, since whole milk and 2% milk are the products the advice asks them to cut back. The call for moderate portions and the call for low-fat products do not rest on equally firm proof, and a reader should know which one to hold tightly.

Recent research has called the low-fat rule into question. Participants in a recent trial ate full-fat dairy three times a day, according to the University of Toronto, and their results did not show the harm the low-fat rule is meant to prevent. "Those that had three servings of dairy didn't have adverse levels of blood cholesterol or lipids or evidence of insulin resistance," said Harvey Anderson, a professor of nutritional sciences at the Temerty Faculty of Medicine at the University of Toronto. Blood pressure also improved in the groups eating dairy, so on that measure the full-fat eaters did better rather than merely no worse. For these markers, choosing low-fat looks more like a precaution against a harm the trial did not find than a proven requirement.

That challenge still leaves moderation standing. The trial lasted only 12 weeks, according to the University of Toronto. That is long enough to show early changes in cholesterol and blood pressure but too short to show what years of full-fat dairy would do. Even the researcher questioning the low-fat rule does not argue for more dairy, since Anderson favors eating many different foods and limiting how much of any one goes on the plate. His view and Mayo Clinic's call for moderate portions point the same way, and the trial's three daily servings were an amount researchers tested, not a license.

Moderation is the rule worth keeping, though Mayo Clinic gives no daily or weekly count for it. Its one concrete gauge is the size of a single serving, which matches four dice, and readers have to judge for themselves how many of those servings still count as moderate.

Keep dairy portions modest even without a set number, and treat low-fat as a sensible default that researchers have reopened for debate, not as a rule to obey at all costs.

The Mediterranean diet asks people to eat less red and processed meat, but it stops short of banning it. Mayo Clinic advises reducing both, and the heart benefit of doing so depends on what takes the meat's place. Among people who ate pulses instead of all their red meat, the risk of ischemic heart disease was 10% lower, according to EMJ. The gain belongs to the trade, not to the cut alone. A plate with the meat simply removed does only half the job, and the empty space needs beans, lentils or other pulses.

The limit worth holding firmly is the one on processed meat. EMJ reports that ischemic heart disease risk ran 15% lower when pulses took the place of processed meat, and cardiovascular disease risk ran 13% lower when they stood in for processed poultry. Both results involve processed foods, whether red meat or poultry, so the common thread is processing rather than the color of the meat. These findings center on processed meat and say nothing on their own about whether fresh cuts carry the same risk. That fits the diet's own guidance, which still allows lean cuts in small portions.

The findings come from a large group of adults in the U.K., 118,272 people in the UK Biobank, according to EMJ. A link of this kind supports the trade, but it doesn't prove that eating pulses instead of processed meat is what lowers the risk.

The practical rule is to be strict about processed meat and to fill the gap with pulses. Lean fresh cuts in small portions are an allowance the diet makes on purpose, not a lapse to feel guilty about.

Eggs sit on the limited side of the Mediterranean diet, and the limit falls on the yolk rather than on the egg as a whole. Mayo Clinic allows up to four whole eggs a week, and it sets that limit because of the saturated fat in the yolk rather than because of any doubt about the egg's nutritional value. For most people the cap asks for little change, since typical intake already runs three to four eggs a week, according to TIME. A reader who follows it mainly avoids drifting toward an omelet every morning.

The heart risk behind the cap is real but small for any one person. In 2019, researchers estimated that an extra half egg a day would mean about 11 additional heart disease cases among every 1,000 people across 30 years, according to TIME. Nearly everyone in that group would see no difference, which makes this an effect that appears across a population rather than a sharp danger to each eater. The four-egg figure is a nutritionists' suggestion rather than a number taken from a study, and the science underneath it is open. "The existing literature is still controversial and inconclusive for nutrition experts and researchers to conclude the safety of eggs," said Victor Wenze Zhong, a postdoctoral fellow at Northwestern University's Feinberg School of Medicine and a co-author of the study behind that estimate. A fifth egg in one week breaks no rule. A steady climb past four, though, moves toward the intake the research flags.

The most useful trade happens inside the egg itself. Mayo Clinic's cap counts whole eggs and targets the fat in the yolk, while egg whites contain no cholesterol, according to TIME. Zhong himself recommends whites as a stand-in for whole eggs. A scramble made with one yolk and several extra whites keeps eggs on the plate without adding to the weekly yolk count.

Be strict about how many yolks you eat and relaxed about how many eggs.

Herbs and spices earn their place mainly for what they let a cook leave out of a dish. Mayo Clinic lists them among the foods the Mediterranean diet is most often high in, which places seasoning among the diet's regular foods rather than its occasional ones. The diet calls for them because a cook who seasons food richly needs less salt. The CDC names spices, garlic and citrus juice as substitutes for salt in cooking, so the swap is shared health advice rather than one diet's quirk. A cook who keeps those on hand has several familiar ways to make food taste full without the shaker.

The swap does less than it seems to, because the saltshaker was never where most of the salt came from. Packaged foods and restaurant meals supply most of the sodium people eat, the CDC reports, and bread adds more because people eat it often even though each serving carries only moderate sodium. Seasoning at home therefore trims only a minor share of a person's salt. Bread is also one of the foods the diet encourages, so a cook who seasons generously still takes in salt that no herb can remove. The full trade has two parts: season richly at home, and cut back on packaged foods, sauces and takeout.

Mayo Clinic sets no serving target for herbs and spices, so a reader has no amount to measure against. That fits the trade itself, because success shows in the salt left out rather than in the amount of seasoning put in.

Here a reader can be as generous as they like, and the strictness belongs to the salt hidden in the rest of their food.

Mormon Wives' Jen Affleck Alleges Zac Affleck Gambled Away Their Money

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County’s Jen Affleck claimed her husband Zac Affleck gambled all of their money, alleging he spent over $100,000 that was in their bank account.

Jen Affleck is an open book about what went wrong in her marriage to Zac Affleck.

Months before the Secret Lives of Mormon Wives star filed for divorce from Zac after seven years of marriage, Jen—who is pregnant with the couple's fourth baby—detailed what led to their breakup on the spinoff series The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County, claiming that her husband gambled away their savings.

And not only that, but Jen alleged he did it numerous times throughout their marriage.

“He was being really weird,” she shared during a conversation with her costar Bobbi Althoff in the fourth episode of the Hulu series, out Nov. 12. “It seemed like he just wasn't being honest. So, I went to check our bank. The money's gone, and there's over $100,000 in there. So, I called him and I'm like, ‘Why is the money gone?’”

“He’s done this before multiple times,” Jen continued, “whether it was through online poker or it was through investing in crypto, I said, ‘You're not allowed to do that anymore.“

Bobbi chimed in, asking an emotional Jen for clarification that Zac has “just spent all of your money” before, to which Jen replied, “Yes.”

Breaking down in tears, the 27-year-old went on to tell Bobbi that Zac “admitted” to emptying their bank account.

“He’s like, ‘I f--ked up and you wanted to separate, so I didn't tell you this because I knew if you found out you would divorce me,’” Jen shared. “And I said, ‘Yeah, I'm done. I'm divorcing you. F--k you. I’m literally pregnant.”

The MomToker added that she only took a closer look at the family’s finances when someone “came to Zac parents’ house yesterday and was trying to serve him papers.”

“They were looking for him,” she recalled. “He's like, ‘Yeah, well, remember we lived in that home in Arizona. I guess I didn't pay all the extra charges or whatever for us to move out.’ And I was like, ‘You told me you took care of that.’”

“Zac and I have moved a lot in the past couple of years,” Jen continued. “We rented a house in Arizona for a year. We ended up moving back to Utah. We literally moved out of that house in Arizona two years ago. So, I thought everything was taken care of. I mean, Zac was handling the finances during that time, but finding this out has made me question everything.”

And, according to the Dancing With the Stars alum, she’s still unsure about the extent of Zac’s alleged debts.

E! News has reached out to Zac for comment on the gambling allegations but has not heard back.

However, the 30-year-old recently addressed his split, sharing in a Nov. 5 TikTok, “Jen came to me about a year ago, after Dancing With the Stars, and told me she was unhappy and wanted a divorce. And I basically begged her to stay, told her work on things, fix things, become whoever she needed me to be."

"I don't want anyone speaking bad about Jen," he continued. "I'll be the first to say I've made plenty of mistakes in our marriage, and it just really was time for us to go our own ways. We got married so young, and it sucks because I really did feel like we had something pretty special when we got married."

Jen and Zac’s marital issues played out on their original Hulu show, with the pair ultimately deciding to move to Orange County for a fresh start. But by the spinoff’s first episode, Jen said she had already hit her “breaking point.”

“I never thought I’d be in the same situation again where I’d be on the verge of divorcing Zac and get pregnant again,” she admitted. “There’s already judgment around divorce when it comes to the church, throw on a pregnancy, and that’s gonna become worse.”

And as Jen enters her new chapter while preparing for the birth of baby no. 4, read on for where the rest of the SLOMW couples stand…

Taylor Frankie Paul & Dakota Mortensen: Broken Up

While Taylor Frankie Paul's romance with Dakota Mortensen—they welcomed son Ever in 2024—bloomed on The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives, they called it quits due to Dakota's infidelity.

"Right after Ever was born, Dakota and I were living together and in bliss with our new baby, and I was even considering engagement," Taylor—who began dating the real estate agent in July 2022—revealed during the season two premiere of the Hulu series. "And then I learned that he was lying this entire time."

Taylor was told by a woman named Jenna that she and Dakota had been intimate and he had "spent the night more than once." (He has denied the claims, writing in a September 2024 TikTok that he "never cheated.")

On the series, Taylor said that Dakota accused Jenna of instigating their affair.

"No, it was him," she emphasized in the confessional. "He said he had just kissed her on the couch. No, he went on the couch, they hooked up on the couch, and then they went to the room and they hooked up."

Since ending her romance with the Basin Tiling founder, the mom of three (she shares two kids with ex-husband Tate Paul) was set to find love once again on season 22 of The Bachelorette, but things got a bit thorny when news broke in March 2026 of an alleged confrontation between the exes weeks earlier.

A spokesperson for the Draper City Police Department confirmed to People that there was an open "domestic assault investigation," telling the outlet "allegations have been made in both directions.”

Days later, a video of a previous 2023 physical altercation between Taylor and Dakota surfaced online, prompting ABC to cancel the MomToker's season of the dating franchise.

As for Dakota, he has kicked off a romance with show guest star Shinia Powell, though he previously apologized to Taylor for hooking up with her during a season four episode.

Whitney Leavitt & Connor Leavitt: Married

Whitney Leavitt found the perfect dance partner in Connor Leavitt, whom she wed in 2016. But it wasn't always smooth sailing for the couple—who share kids Sedona, Liam and Billy—as they navigated life in the spotlight.

For instance, the Dancing With the Stars alum, who announced during her Broadway run in May 2026 that she'd be leaving Mormon Wives, had to shut down rumors about her husband's sexuality.

"Do I need to put out a sex tape, or something? Like, trust me," she said on the Off the Vine podcast in October 2024. "We're good. Everything's fine."

"I think it's because when we were going through our personal stuff—and I know you haven't seen it—but he struggled through a porn addiction," she explained. "There's a scene where one of my friends says something along the lines of how they could see how he was confused sexually."

Through the ups and downs, the reality star emphasized that she and Connor—who were 23 and 22, respectively, when they got married—are stronger than ever. In fact, they even renewed their vows in February 2026 for their 10th wedding anniversary.

"We got married really young," Conner told Brides after their ceremony. "It felt like our wedding day, as much as it was for us, it really felt like it was for our families. So we really want to do something to make it ours, and this felt like a really fun way to do it."

Married in 2019, Jen Affleck and Zac Affleck—no relation to Ben Affleck, despite jokes to the contrary—have been at the center of breakup rumors since SLOMW's debut.

In fact, their fight after Jen attended a Chippendales striptease show in Las Vegas became a focal point of the first season. The couple—who share kids Nora, Penelope and Lucas—made up, however, and the Dancing With the Stars contestant later defended their relationship.

"It can be tiring and draining, just because it gets old having to hear the same things," Jen told E! News of the speculations in November 2025. "For me, I'm like, 'People will always talk and people will always have their opinions.' I think it is frustrating for him when people don't see what's happening behind closed doors and how much work and effort he's putting in."

But while Jen announced in June 2026 that she'd be adding to their dance troupe with baby No. 4, the MomToker filed for divorce that November after seven years of marriage.

Jessi Draper & Jordan Ngatikaura: Divorced

Bumps in Jessi Draper and Jordan Ngatikaura's five-year marriage emerged after the hairstylist admitted to having an emotional affair with Vanderpump Villa's Marciano Brunette, though initially it appeared they were working towards smoother roads.

"We are back together," Jessi—who shares kids Jagger and Jovi with Jordan—told E! News in November 2025. "We're trying to work on it. Relationships are not perfect. I think a situation like ours—it's going to take time. It's not going to something that's just amazing overnight."

Sadly, the reconciliation appeared to be short-lived, with Jordan also dad to daughter Peyton from a previous relationship, filing for divorce in March 2026 after five years of marriage.

However, days later, Jessi said she was “blindsided” by his filing and accused him of rushing to submit the paperwork because “he wanted the headline first.”

Admitting that her “heart sunk” seeing the news, she explained, “because we agreed to tell our children together before filing and we haven’t done that.” (E! reached out to attorneys for Jordan for comment but haven’t heard back.)

Though Jessi told E! News that she was "just casually dating and living life," when she brought Marciano as her date to the 2026 American Music Awards in May 2026, he revealed a tattoo dedicated to her that August.

Jessi and Jordan finalized their divorce in September 2026.

Mayci didn't expect to find love with Jacob Neeley. In fact, after the unexpected death of her boyfriend Arik Mack in 2015, when she was just 14 weeks pregnant with their son Hudson, she was prepared to be a single mom.

But it all changed when she met Jacob in 2016 at age 21, whom she would marry two years later.

"After everything I'd been through, I was very guarded, but Jacob was kind and we got on really well," Mayci said in her memoir Told You So. "For a long time, we remained just friends and because we weren't really dating, I was OK with him meeting my son."

"Jacob was amazing with him, which made him appear even more attractive," she continued. "I was terrified of getting hurt again but, after four months, I couldn't deny my feelings any longer. He was kind and funny and made me feel safe."

Mayci and Jacob went on to welcome daughters Harlow in 2021 and Charli in 2025.

Demi Engemann and her husband Bret Engemann tied the knot in 2021, one year after she and Blake Corbin divorced. As the couple's relationship played out on the reality show, their sexual explorations became a major plot point after Whitney gifted Demi a box of Fruity Pebbles.

The truth behind the joke gift wasn't revealed until Jessi shared on Instagram in November 2025 that "grandpa Bret likes to drink her piss like it's dirty soda and he gulps it down like water."

But Demi and Bret aren't crying over spilled secrets—or pee. In fact, she told Entertainment Tonight that December that she and Bret "have nothing else left to do other than own it and have fun with it."

Although their sex life raised eyebrows, the couple has been embracing life with their blended family. While Demi shares daughter Maude with her ex, Bret is dad to his and The Real Housewives of Salt Lake City alum Angie Harrington's sons Rome and Cole.

"I've never seen a dad so involved or love their kids harder, the most devoted step-dad to Maude, and future father to our own children one day," Demi wrote on Instagram in June 2024. "Your ability to remain consistently loyal to your role as father/step-father while navigating all that life throws your way, will never cease to amaze me!"

Mikayla Matthews and Jace Terry have been through it all together. While The reality star first met Jace in 2015 when she was 15 and they welcomed their first son Beckham two years later. Since then, the couple—who tied the knot in 2018—welcomed kids Haven, Tommie and Lottie.

While Mikayla and Jace have been loving life with their four kids, Mikayla admitted that she hopes to grow their family even further.

"When people ask me how many kids I want, I'm like, 'I've always wanted three or four, but I could see myself having six,'" she told People in February 2025. "I'm excited to hopefully move into a new house. I want a big lot for my herd of kids."

However, Mikayla confirmed in the ninth episode of the show's fourth season that she and Jace were taking time apart amid ongoing intimacy struggles.

As Mikayla began unpacking her trauma surrounding years of sexual abuse as a teen, as well as her chronic illness—which causes flare-ups due to heightened stress—their marriage had been deeply affected.

But the tides have officially turned on their connection as the reality star shared during the show's fifth season that she and Jace are back on track.

"I feel like I got a lot more clarity," she said in a confessional on the show, "and so I was able to express more what I needed to Jace."

And, as she added, “He’s been very supportive.”

After Layla Taylor and Clayton Wessel—who are parents to sons Oliver and Maxwell—divorced in 2023, the reality star teased her new romance with Mason McWhorter in July 2025.

Their relationship was a bit complicated on paper. Hang with us: Mason's brother is Chase McWhorter, the ex-husband of Layla's costar Miranda McWhorter. But the pair had been willing to put their romance to the test.

"Right now, I'm kind of scared of marriage but I just want to get to know somebody very well before I even consider that," Layla told Us in November 2025. "I'm just happy and content where me and Mason are. And if somehow that ever were to come up, then we would see."

Unfortunately, the mom of two confirmed that she and Mason had split in February 2026.

"I think we were just on two different paths right now, and he felt in order for him to grow, he had to be on his own," Layla shared. "I respect his decision, and I truly hope that he gets what he wants in life."

As for Layla, who came out as bisexual in June 2026, she soft-launched her romance with a mystery woman a month later in an Instagram video. She later seemed to confirm her relationship with Colorado-based content creator Kelsie Chesser.

Miranda McWhorter & Chase McWhorter: Divorced

Although Miranda is a newer addition to The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives, joining in season two, she played a major role in the infamous MomTok "soft swinging scandal." Despite the situation with Taylor, Miranda revealed that drama wasn't the reason for her 2024 breakup from Chase.

"The reasons why we decided to split were things that were issues far before that,” she explained on the Weekly Trash podcast in August 2024. "That might not have helped, for sure, but it definitely did not play a part in the decision process."

Nonetheless, she and Chase have been committed to parenting their kids Brooks and Cohen.

"With co-parenting, you're always having to work on it," she reflected. “The thing is that if we got along perfectly all the time and it was just rainbows and butterflies, we probably wouldn't have gotten divorced."

"The thing with him and I," she continued, "we've always had a pretty good foundation of a friendship prior to even having a romantic relationship. I feel like that's something that set us up for some level of success when it comes to co-parenting."

Though Miranda and Chase broke up in 2024, the former filed for divorce in 2026.

‘Ebenezer’ Review: Johnny Depp Is Absolutely Insufferable in a Dull and Dreary Christmas Carol

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A Sauvage and jowly overactor in dire need of career rehabilitation could hardly ask for a better part to play than Ebenezer Scrooge. He’s a cruel miser of a man who comes to learn the error of his ways over the course of a ghost story that preys upon Christian precepts of redemption and forgiveness (along with the more secular warm and fuzzies of seeing a bad guy discover the joys of being good). Indeed, what is “cancel culture” if not a shackled delusion that visits self-made pariahs in the dead of night, and what is Charles Dickens’ “A Christmas Carol” if not a plea to believe that even the most odious of souls are still capable of being saved? Related Stories Sandra Wollner’s ‘Everytime’ Is a Stunning Film About Grief Like You’ve Never Seen Before How ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Dominated the Box Office: GKIDS Chief on IMAX’s Impact and the Growing Market for Foreign-Language Movies

It’s been reported that Johnny Depp accepted the title role of Ti West’s “Ebenezer” at a fraction of his usual rate, but I suspect that he probably would have done this movie for free. While the embattled star earned more than $250 million for his work on the “Pirates of the Caribbean” franchise, even that psychotic number pales in comparison to the pricelessness of starring in a major studio Christmas movie that allows him to look just left of the camera and say lines like “I’m not the man I was!” and “I was once good, and I know I can be again!” before his character saves a cherubic little boy from certain death and invites a freezing beggar inside for a warm cup of tea. If an old crank like Scrooge can make his way back toward grace, why not the convicted star of “Mortdecai,” and the alleged perpetrator of several even more serious crimes?

Johnny Depp may prefer for that to be framed as a rhetorical question, but his insufferable turn as literature’s most cantankerous moneylender demands an honest answer. For me, that answer is rather straightforward: While Depp has never been the subtlest of actors, his post-Sparrow pivot away from artistic curiosity has distended his talents into such a grotesque caricature of their former charm that “Ebenezer” left me feeling like I may never be able to empathize with his screen presence again.

Scrooge is obviously a scenery-chewer’s dream, but the crescent arc he traces from grinch to Christmas miracle is only believable enough to sustain Dickens’ fable if you believe it on both ends. Depp’s performance — an advent calendar of empty affectations without a soul to anchor them, every shot full of face and bereft of feeling — makes that exceedingly difficult. His offscreen unsavoriness certainly doesn’t help, but there’s a more immediate problem when the lights go down, one that makes him a unique case among Hollywood’s tainted array of mega-star actors: He seems to have become very bad at acting. Whatever my feelings about Depp as a human being, the truth is that I didn’t buy him as a glowering monster any more than I bought him as a beaming man who’s grown “better than his word.”

An unengaging Ebenezer is a tough break for a movie called “Ebenezer,” and screenwriter Nathaniel Halpern’s fidelity to the source material doesn’t exactly leave West with a wealth of opportunities to compensate for such fatal miscasting. West’s take on the holiday classic is faithful to the core — no Muppets, no ghosts of girlfriends past, no uncanny mo-capped Jim Carrey. As you might expect from the director of indie genre standouts like “Pearl” and “The House of the Devil,” West’s big idea is to scare the Dickens out of us by turning up the volume on this story’s implicit scariness. Sometimes literally, as this PG-13 carol offers a small handful of gentle jump-scares.

“Ebenezer” isn’t a horror movie in the strictest sense, but West does what he can to play up the fear that eventually terrifies Scrooge into giving a shit about his fellow man. Most — or all — of those flourishes are purely cosmetic in nature, and hellbent on sucking every glimmer of life out of Victorian England. Scrooge’s office has never looked more haunted, as Checco Varese’s grimdark cinematography and Eve Stewart’s Yharnam-esque production design combine to create a London that’s in dire need of Christmas cheer (the vibe should go a long way with tweenage audiences eager for a bit of spice with their holiday viewing, though I suspect the whole thing is too staid and oppressive to become any kind of perennial favorite).

And since West would sooner die than pass up a chance for death, this version of the story treats us to a musty prologue where we actually get to see Ebenezer’s business partner bite the dust with his mouth wide open (Marley is played by Ian McKellen in what currently stands as the most “remember ‘The Christophers?’” performance of the year). From there, the action skips forward to Christmas Eve 1843, whence Ebenezer is inflicting his usual “Bah, humbug!” routine on poor Bob Cratchit (Rupert Grint), the underpaid clerk he resents for taking the next day off (HR being a decidedly 20th-century invention). But the fates have plans of their own for the bitter-cold businessman, who promptly pisses himself when the ghost of old Marley bursts into his miserable bedchamber later that night.

Reaction shots ensue, as Depp curdles his face into a series of the most exaggerated expressions since the death of silent cinema, every one of them betraying his character’s frigidness. This Ebenezer takes an active pleasure in being cruel, which not only makes him more detestable (which is fine), but also endows him with an innate capacity for joy that makes the rest of the movie feel like it’s walking in circles (which is less fine). Given that Depp’s performance is only as wide as his lips and as deep as his pockets, it seems wasteful that we should have to walk through the hollows of Ebenezer’s past, present, and future in order to fix him — it would be easier and equally meaningful to just push the corners of his mouth into a smile.

Of course, that would deprive us of the “holy shit, that’s Andrea Riseborough” fun of the “To Leslie” star’s Ghost of Christmas Past, a white-eyed ghoul who looks like a spirit from “The Conjuring” who popped up for a sudden jolt and then forgot to leave the frame. She’s the most amusing part of a familiar tour through the sadness of Ebenezer’s childhood, where he was bullied by the other kids and kept apart from his beloved older sister. Tramell Tillman is equally compelling as the boisterous Ghost of Christmas Present, though his bizarre character design — a round-bellied man whose face appears to be rotting to pieces in real-time — turns out to be reverse-engineered from an unnerving reveal that both honors Dickens’ text and also underlines West’s limited conviction to rejuvenate it with the visceral immediacy of a horror film (his commitment to the bit falls well short of the Robert Eggers-level follow-through that conceit demands).

Given the emphasis the director puts on the film’s genre spin, it’s ironic and surprising that “Ebenezer” is at its best when it focuses on the most basic human elements of its story, if only because the supporting characters offer such a welcome contrast to whatever Depp is doing. Sam Claflin and Daisy Ridley don’t have a whole lot to do as Ebenezer’s kind-hearted nephew Fred and his wife Clara (respectively), but the film takes pains to stress how sad it is that Scrooge is Fred’s last connection to his dead mother, and it’s touching to see how much it would mean for him to have a cordial relationship with the old miser.

It is considerably less touching to see Ebenezer arrive at a mutual feeling after palling around with the film’s Nazgûl-coded Ghost of Christmas Yet to Come (Robert Strange, cloaked in CGI), who forces the misanthrope to face the emptiness of his own miserable existence. “The flame of humanity still flickers within you,” someone tells Scrooge, but the slapstick pageantry of Depp’s performance sabotages the movie by keeping that flame at a low simmer from the start. I didn’t believe the fire had ever gone out any more than I believed that it was eventually rekindled, as this Ebenezer Scrooge is far too elastic and self-amused to convincingly suggest that he’s experiencing a permanent change of heart. He can’t even sustain a facial expression for more than a few seconds before he starts mugging around in search of another one.

There will, of course, be those who insist that I only panned “Ebenezer” because Johnny Depp is in it, and I suppose they’re at least half-right about that, if not quite for the reason they assume. The truth is that it would be a lot more interesting and true to the spirit of Dickens’ classic if I liked this movie in spite of Depp rather than disliking it because of him, as I can’t help but share the author’s opinion that everyone is theoretically capable of redemption, be it measure by measure or miraculously complete.

Alas, the actor’s “comeback” performance is just yet another thing that he ought to be sorry for.

Paramount Pictures will release “Ebenezer” in theaters on Friday, November 13.

Mortgage Rates Fall for Fourth Straight Week Despite Looming Fed Hike

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Mortgage rates continued their slow retreat for a fourth consecutive week, mirroring the trajectory of bond yields, although this week's hotter-than-expected inflation print suggests the relief will be short-lived.

The average rate on 30-year fixed home loans decreased to 7.11% for the week ending Nov. 12, down 7 basis points from 7.18% the week before, according to Freddie Mac. For perspective, rates averaged 6.24% one year ago.

"Mortgage rates dropped to their lowest level in seven weeks, a welcome development that gives borrowers greater flexibility as they navigate the current housing market," says Sam Khater, Freddie Mac's chief economist.

A month of falling mortgage rates comes as welcome news for homebuyers, though Realtor.com® economist Jiayi Xu warns that a reversal could come as soon as next week.

The stronger-than-forecast October jobs report showed the labor market holding up well, clearing the way for the Federal Reserve's next federal funds rate hike as part of its mandate to curb inflation.

Fed Governor Christopher Waller warned this week that a larger 50 basis point increase should be considered in December’s Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) meeting if inflation remains hot.

Shortly after Waller's warning, the October Consumer Price Index report exceeded economists' expectations, pushing the 10-year Treasury yield toward 5%.

A December hike is now essentially priced in, with odds of a 25-basis-point increase topping 93%, according to CME FedWatch. The odds of another quarter-percentage-point hike in January 2027 currently stand at 67%.

"The debate is no longer if the Fed hikes, but how far and how fast," says Xu.

Today's homebuyers find themselves squeezed by both high mortgage rates and high inflation, and Xu contends that inflation is the bigger long-term problem.

"High rates raise monthly payments and shrink the loan a buyer can qualify for, and the recent pullback in pending sales shows how sensitive buyers are," notes the economist. "But persistent high inflation is what keeps the Fed hiking and rates elevated."

Crucially, inflation also erodes households' purchasing power, especially when wages lag consumer prices.

In October, existing-home sales dropped to their lowest level since Aug. 2010, according to the latest data  from the National Association of Realtors®.

In this economic environment, first-time buyers are hit the hardest because they have no existing equity to use as a cushion, forcing them to save toward a down payment from already-stretched budgets.

"Even if rates ease, living costs rising faster than pay will keep weighing on demand for years," says Xu.

Sellers have responded to cooling buyer demand by pulling back. This week, new listing activity fell 10.3% year over year after six weeks of more incremental declines, according to the latest weekly Realtor.com housing trends report.

"Sellers are discouraged because of how disengaged buyers are," says Realtor.com senior economist Hannah Jones. "The prices buyers are open to are less appealing to sellers than they were when mortgage rates were lower."

Your credit score plays a role when you apply for a mortgage. A credit score will determine whether you qualify for a mortgage and the interest rate you'll receive. The higher the credit score, the lower the interest rate you'll qualify for.

The credit score you need will vary depending on the type of loan. A score of 620 is a "fair" rating. However, people applying for a Federal Housing Administration loan might be able to get approved with a credit score of 500, which is considered a low score.

Homebuyers with credit scores of 740 or higher are typically considered to be in very good standing and can usually qualify for better rates, which can reduce monthly payments.

Snejana Farberov is a reporter at Realtor.com covering the U.S. housing market and the latest domestic real estate trends. She has worked as a general assignment journalist in New York City and Long Island for 16 years, writing for New York Post, Daily Mail, and News 12. Snejana earned bachelor's degrees in journalism and Italian from St. John's University, followed by a master’s degree from Columbia University School of Journalism.

NFL predictions: Our picks for Giants-Commanders, the full Week 10 slate

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The New York Giants and Washington Commanders kick off Week 10 of the NFL season Thursday night at what promises to be a rainy, chilly MetLife Stadium. Can the Giants solidify their NFC East lead, or will the race for the division title tighten? Here is how the Big Blue View staff sees that game, and the rest of the Week 10 matchups.

Which version of the Giants will show up on Thursday night?

Will we get the sloppy Giants that came out flat against the Philadelphia Eagles, or the tough, physical, and technically sound Giants that showed up against the Houston Texans? John Harbaugh's 9-1 record home record on Thursday Nights is encouraging.

I would say that this is a game the Giants should win on paper, particularly with Washington missing some of their best players and their starting center in the concussion protocol. However, I'm also not convinced that these Giants can play a normal game and just take care of business. I keep waiting for the Giants to play a complete game and execute on offense and defense, but this team seems unable to be both consistent and explosive.

I hope the offensive line comes prepared to bully an injured Washington defensive line and J.J. McCarthy isn't forced into low percentage throws every third down by long yardage situations. Likewise, I hope that Dennard Wilson comes out swinging on defense, but also has a counterpunch ready in case Jayden Daniels has an answer to the Cover-0 blitz.

I'm picking the Giants here, but they can't afford to take Washington lightly or give Jayden Daniels the chance to catch his breath and swing the game.

This is the Giants' second consecutive must-win game. You wouldn't think that when 5-2-1 plays 2-5-1, but (1) the Giants haven't beaten the Commanders since Jayden Daniels became their quarterback (even though Daniels didn't play in one of those losses), (2) despite their record, Washington has beaten the Seahawks and only narrowly lost to the 49ers and Rams while the Giants were beaten soundly by the Rams, the only good team they've faced, and still have the 49ers and Seahawks coming up, along with the Jaguars and Lions. A division loss greatly weakens their hold on the NFC East lead, their only real path to the playoffs.

Terry McLaurin is out for the season, but in his place is Stefon Diggs, who did not face the Giants in the first game. Jacory Crosky-Merritt, who beat the Giants' run D for a 33-yard TD right off the bat in the first game, is also out. The Commanders are further decimated by injuries since their last meeting to key starters such as DT Daron Payne and IOL Lucas Patrick. So really the Giants should prevail. The problem is…containing the running of Daniels, which they did not do well in their first meeting (103 yards, by far his highest of the season, and his only 2 rushing TDs of the season). Hopefully Dennard Wilson will have a plan for that. If so, and if the Giants execute it, then the Giants can win.

The rematch of the NFL's only tie game of the season will be played in prime time on Thursday night, and for the ninth time in nine games, the Giants are playing a team that doesn't have a winning record. But you can throw the records out the window: The 5-2-1 Giants, at home, are just 1.5 point favorites over the 2-5-1 Commanders. Go figure…

Big Blue won with defense and a strong kicking game on Sunday in Philadelphia, with Arvel Reese perhaps becoming a star in real time. It was an ugly win, with the offense producing just 19 points. The Giants will need more than that against Jayden Daniels and company on Thursday night. Cam Skattebo is expected back, and that's good news. New York couldn't run the ball on Sunday, which stagnated their offense. Right tackle Jermaine Eluemunor (calf) might miss this game, however, and the O-line struggled at Philadelphia as it was.

J.J. McCarthy wasn't terrible on Sunday, but he wasn't especially good either and now he's dealing with a hip pointer. Jameis Winston led New York on its only touchdown drive whileMcCarthy was temporarily knocked from the game. McCarthy hasn't thrown a TD pass (or an interception) in either of his two starts.

This figures to be a close game, just like the first one. It'll be fun to watch standout rookies Reese and Sonny Styles — college teammates at Ohio State last season — leading the way for each defense. Seriously – how did anyone score on the Buckeyes? Anyway, Dennard Wilson needs to find a way to keep Daniels from gashing his defense with his legs. He ran for 103 yards and two scores in the first game.

This game could come down to intangibles like turnovers and special teams. The G-Men have been winning (or tying) close games and that's a reflection on good coaching to a degree, and favorable luck. In a game that should be close, who am I to pick against that?

We are not picking games by the spread here, but I am surprised the Giants are just 1.5-point favorites. That indicates a continuing lack of real belief in the Giants in the betting market. The Giants understand the opportunity they have in front of them, and how important this game is.

In the rainy, windy, miserable conditions at MetLife Stadium Thursday evening I think you have to expect what has become a typical Giants game this season — close, somewhat sloppy, decided by who makes the plays and who properly handles the situations at the end of the game.

I have far more faith in the Giants to do that than I do the Commanders.

We all remember the Commanders stunning clock mismanagement at the end of the Week 5 tie with the Giants. That sort of questionable decision-making has continued to plague Washington, which is 1-3-1 in games decided by one score.

If you could the Arizona game, which I do since it was decided on the game's final play, seven of the Giants eight games this season were one-score games. The Giants are 5-1-1 in those games. They have lived on the edge, and have shown an ability to survive.

Is that formula sustainable forever? Maybe not. It does, though, make me feel good about the Giants' chances on Thursday.

Burnout is a workplace safety hazard. It’s time to start treating it like one

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Burnout is a huge workplace issue: the low engagement that comes with it costs every business affected a fortune. Worse yet, its effects on workers are deleterious to the point where it can literally lead to death.

Yet in my experience, most leaders consider “burnout” an HR line item to be managed by a wellness program. It’s hard to know how to jolt people out of this mindset, but if I were a CEO (and I am), I’d be very concerned about potential industrial manslaughter charges coming my way at worst, and shareholders rightfully wondering why I am failing to manage the main thing that’s materially impairing productivity at best.

Burnout is a workplace safety hazard—and needs to be governed as one. 

The World Health Organization defines burnout as the result of “chronic workplace stress that has not been successfully managed.” It’s a costly hazard that claims at least twice as many lives as workplace accidents. Long working hours alone led to an estimated 750,000 deaths from stroke and heart disease in 2016, more than twice the 360,000 people killed by workplace injuries, according to a report from WHO and the International Labour Organization. In the USA, workplace stressors are estimated to kill 120,000 people a year. 

Outside of the devastating human impact, the toll burnout takes on commercial performance should be triggering emergency board meetings all over the world. At an estimated cost of US $5 million a year for a company of 1,000 employees, it would be reasonable to ask very direct questions of the executive about their plans to arrest the poorly designed workplace practices that lead to burnout. 

Maybe the questions could start with who decided that it was okay to spend more than half of your working week in meetings or managing the almost 600 emails and more than 750 Teams messages knowledge workers field every week. Perhaps some questions about what the executive has done to deliberately design the working week toward outcomes—not admin. 

Burnout and chronic workplace stress come from the health of a company’s culture, and culture is almost universally dismissed, neglected or delegated to HR: A function with no formal training in organizational culture, no resources to shape it, and by its very station, no authority over the leadership behavior that does.

So, when an organization is faced with a growing number of employees taking chronic work stress-related leave—in Australia, the median time off for a serious mental health claim is almost five times that of other serious workplace injuries—it looks for a button to press, inside a function it believes has the answer. But doesn’t. 

HR has never had the answer and never will. The organizations that shape their culture with intention, and deliberately look at the accidental practices we’ve inherited from antique cultures, don’t start with HR. They look directly at:

  • the board, which needs to be asking the right questions, in line with increasing regulation and on behalf of shareholders looking for optimal productivity
  • the CEO, who is entirely accountable for the productivity and performance of the organization
  • the leaders who shape culture through the expectations they set, the practices they perpetuate, the consequences they apply for culture drift, and the chronic workplace issues they walk past every single day

CEOs and organizations never set out to burn their people out. It’s more a matter of ‘out of sight, out of mind’ and neglect. Neglecting the implications of workplace practices, neglecting better ways of working that are confirmed by research rather than the assumptions of a post-industrial era, and neglecting to look through the human lens at a growing contributor to cardiovascular incidents and death by suicide.

Whether leaders look at burnout through a productivity lens or from a human suffering perspective, the situation is clear. Leaders are enabling the ongoing, systemic impairment of commercial performance at the cost of human lives…and feeling OK about it because they’ve passed a memo to HR to invest in a wellness program or resilience training.

Thankfully, regulators and standard setters understand the crisis better and have begun something of a reckoning. ISO 45003 became the first international standard for managing psychological health and safety at work in 2021, and the US Surgeon General’s Framework for Workplace Mental Health and Well-Being names protection from harm, psychological safety included, as its first essential.

Australia has moved from guidance to enforcement. Every Australian state and territory now requires employers to identify and manage psychosocial hazards in the same way as physical ones, and in December 2025, Australia’s Department of Defence became the first federal employer convicted for failing to manage psychosocial risk.

Burnout is, quite literally, an existential risk. It’s time organizations truly started acting like it.

If this article raises concerns for you or someone you know, support is available. In the USA, call or text the 988 Suicide & Crisis Lifeline on 988. In Australia, call Lifeline on 13 11 14.

The Best Rain Jackets for Hikers Who Always Overheat

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

Picture this: You’re halfway through a 20-mile day when the sky opens up, dumping sheets of rain on you and your hiking partners. You pull out your shell, zip it up, and keep crushing. An hour later, you notice you’re starting to feel awfully damp. Your shell isn’t leaking, but it might as well be: Your exertion has turned it into a sweatbox.

The best rain jackets are next to useless if you end up sweating them out. If you’re the kind of hiker who always steams up your shell, picking one that has the breathability and venting options to keep you cool and dry is a must. We went through our testers’ favorite rain shells of 2026 and chose the best picks for hikers who always overheat.

Weight: 15.3 oz (men’s) / 13.9 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s S-XXXL / Women’s XXS-4X

Pros and Cons + Very breathable + Slightly stretchy + Certified carbon neutral – Too slim for layering

Our favorite rain jacket on the market today is also the most breathable we’ve tested: After wearing the Aspire (the Foray is the men’s equivalent) on a steep, 7-mile hike into a cabin in Alaska’s Chugach National Forest, tester Kate Paskievitch reported that she stayed dry the entire time—even with 40 pounds on her back. Outdoor Research’s AscentShell Dry, a 40-denier, 100-percent recycled three-layer nylon fabric, helped, but credit mostly goes to the full-length side zippers, which helped us bleed off heat quickly.

Weight: 10.9 oz (men’s) / 9.9 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s XS-XXL / Women’s XS-XXL

Pros and Cons + Excellent adjustability + Superior breathability + Dialed-in details – Expensive

Let’s acknowledge the elephant in the room first: Any rain jacket that costs $600 had better be really good. Fortunately, this model from Canada-based 7mesh delivers. Diagonal pit zips kept us from sweating out the Guardian Apex, even when our testers’ arms were down. And the nylon face fabric was both surprisingly breathable and tough enough that it remained snag-free, even after a long bushwhack through brambles.

Weight: 5.8 oz (men’s) / 5.5 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s S-XXL / Women’s XS-XL

Pros and Cons + Very light and packable + Partially plant-based – No hand pockets

At just 5.8 ounces for a men’s medium—roughly the same as the ever-popular Frogg Toggs Ultra Lite—the Marmot SuperAlloy Bio takes up barely any space, making it an ideal just-in-case piece to tuck into your pack. And it performs, too: The 2.5-layer NanoPro fabric kept our tester dry even after she wore it under a heavy pack in 50-degree temperatures, but was still tough enough that it didn’t fray after heavy use.

Weight: 25.4 oz (men’s) / 25.0 oz (women’s) Sizes: Men’s S-XXL / Women’s XS-3XL

Pros and Cons + Soft and supple + Lots of venting options + Easy to repair and actually recyclable – Heavy and bulky – Too warm for summer weather

The Velez Adventure Smock is not the lightest jacket in our test: The jacket, which eschews a waterproof-breathable membrane for a DWR-treated polyester face backed by wicking fabric, is better suited for colder-season temperatures. So what makes it a good jacket for hikers who run hot? In shoulder-season conditions, it breathes and maintains its waterproofing better than most traditional three-layer rain shells. Venting helps, too: Biceps zippers and a horizontal front flap across the torso make it easy to dump heat quickly before users get too steamy.

Weight: 1.8 oz (men’s) Sizes: Men’s XS-XXL

Pros and Cons + Ultralight + Surprisingly durable + Surprisingly weatherproof – No hood or chest pocket – Tight fit

At 1.8 ounces, this gossamer hoodless jacket is a reliable friend for mountain runners, fastpackers, and anyone who values velocity. The 10-denier recycled nylon exterior packs up to (literally) pocket size, and while we wouldn’t bet on it to keep you bone-dry through a multi-day storm, it handled surprise desert downpours without batting an eye. Breathability is exceptional, which our tester confirmed after a full day of biking along the Grand Canyon’s South Rim.

First, understand you may have to compromise. While waterproof-breathable membranes are almost ubiquitous, most jackets prioritize waterproofing over breathability, or vice versa. Jackets that can weather a downpour without so much as wetting out may not breathe well, and those that breathe well may be better for scattered showers than the kind of rain that makes you start thinking about building an ark.

Pay attention not just to the breathability of the jacket itself, but to the venting options, too. Even the most breathable fabrics can’t dump heat as well as a set of pit zips or mesh-backed pockets that expose your underlayers to fresh air. Packability is more important than you might think, too: If you’re consistently hot, having a jacket that you can ball up and stick inside your pack without a second thought is a better move than carrying a heavy, bulky one.

Our lead testers for men’s and women’s rain jackets are Drew Zieff and Elizabeth Kwak. Our gear editors pulled these recommendations from their original testing notes and scores to recommend the best rain jackets for hikers who overheat.

Hyatt Hotel Promises Jonathan Adler Toiletries—But There’s Drugstore Shampoo On Its Housekeeping Cart

Hotel Avante in Mountain View, California advertises Jonathan Adler bath amenities. A reader photographed its housekeeping cart with a retail bottle of HASK shampoo resting among unbranded squeeze bottles. This is another JdV by Hyatt property, just a few miles from the Wild Palms hotel where I reported on DRIFT bulk toiletries on housekeeping carts while the guest bathroom bottles displayed Jonathan Adler branding. Apparently readers need to keep checking what’s on the carts. The brown bottle is HASK Tea Tree Oil & Rosemary Invigorating Shampoo, in the 12-ounce size. HASK is an affordable retail hair care brand, with products sold at CVS and other stores. Around it are condiment-style squeeze bottles. At least one has “Body wash” handwritten on tape. The bathroom had Jonathan Adler bottles, while the housekeeping cart carried large jugs of DRIFT Hydrated Body Care. Topics on this pageHotel AvanteHyattJonathan AdlerCaliforniaCVS Pharmacy+2 more More From View from the Wing

This is another JdV by Hyatt property, just a few miles from the Wild Palms hotel where I reported on DRIFT bulk toiletries on housekeeping carts while the guest bathroom bottles displayed Jonathan Adler branding. Apparently readers need to keep checking what’s on the carts.

The brown bottle is HASK Tea Tree Oil & Rosemary Invigorating Shampoo, in the 12-ounce size. HASK is an affordable retail hair care brand, with products sold at CVS and other stores. Around it are condiment-style squeeze bottles. At least one has “Body wash” handwritten on tape. That identifies the type of product, without telling guests whose product it is.

It seems like Hyatt a good reason to check what this hotel is using to refill its toiletries. But they had this reason at its sister property, too. There needs to be much stronger monitoring.

Hyatt’s Hotel Avante page lists “Jonathan Adler bath amenities” across all six room categories. The hotel’s separate website promises that as well.

I have no particular opinion on HASK shampoo. My issue is that a hotel should deliver the product they specifically advertise to guests. And if the bulk toiletries in the room say they’re a specific brand, they should be that brand.

Sures describes Avante as Wild Palms’ sister property and they’re both part of the same ownership group. In November 2025, I published a reader’s photos from Wild Palms. The bathroom had Jonathan Adler bottles, while the housekeeping cart carried large jugs of DRIFT Hydrated Body Care.

Then in January, Ben at One Mile at a Time published follow-up photos showing the bulk jugs with their labels removed. Wild Palms still advertises Jonathan Adler toiletries.

I just wrote about the ketchup, mustard and mayonnaise bottle approach to hotel toiletries. Once the hotel keeps reusing the branded packaging, it can change the contents without changing anything the guest sees. An owner who substitutes a cheaper product keeps the savings, while Hyatt and the advertised toiletry company risk their reputations. Remind me again why bulk dispensers were a better idea than single use biodegradable packaging?
