Measles outbreaks in 2026: the key numbers from the CDC's case tracker

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The CDC counted 5,182 U.S. measles cases by Nov. 5, 2026. Nine key numbers show who is sick, who is hospitalized and what is at stake.

Most Americans think of measles as a disease from an earlier era, one that vaccines dealt with long before their own children started school. For most of this century, the record supported that view. Between 2000 and 2024, the worst year was 2019, with 1,282 cases by one count from the CDC, although the agency's figures for that year differ slightly from page to page. Even that peak was small for a nation this size, and it showed measles as a rare, contained event until very recently. Against that baseline, the past two years stand out. Case counts climbed quickly, and they have not fallen back to the old level. The 2026 numbers keep raising one question: whether the country is living through a bad stretch or watching measles settle back in to stay.

More is at stake than a grim tally, because the country's standing as a place where measles no longer circulates is under formal review. A decision from the Pan American Health Organization on that elimination status is expected next week, according to Reuters. NBC 26 reports that Canada lost its own status last year. For the U.S., losing the status is a near and concrete possibility with a precedent next door. No ruling has been issued, so nothing is lost yet. The open question is whether the numbers already show the country failing the test. Whatever the ruling, those numbers show how far protection has slipped in parts of the country. That slippage matters more to families than any official label, because it decides where measles can spread next.

The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention built its measles tracker to show how large U.S. measles activity was in 2026 and what that activity was made of. Its figures show who is catching the disease, how many patients end up in the hospital, and how far vaccination among kindergartners has slipped. Read together, the figures do more than tally a bad year. They trace measles through the people left without protection, and the slides that follow pick up that thread.

The U.S. has recorded more measles cases in 2026 than in all of 2025, with about two months of the year still to count. Health officials confirmed 5,182 cases by Nov. 5, more than double the total for all of last year, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Officials reached that mark before the year was out, while the 2025 figure covers a full 12 months. They can only add cases from here, which makes "more than double" the minimum for the year rather than its final result.

A large part of that record comes from just two states. Pennsylvania has counted 1,487 cases, according to state health department figures reported by Reuters, and South Carolina has recorded 670. Pennsylvania's count comes from the state rather than the CDC, but it places a large part of the national record inside a single state. Together, those two states carry a major share of this year's cases, and the rise has hit some parts of the country far harder than others.

Almost everyone who caught measles in 2026 lives in the U.S. International visitors account for only 18 of the more than 5,000 confirmed cases. Measles is easy to picture as something travelers bring in from abroad, but this year's cases come from outbreaks inside American communities.

For anyone weighing their own risk, gaps in local vaccination matter far more than who crosses the border.

Nearly every measles case in the U.S. this year has been tied to an outbreak. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, 96% of 2026 cases were linked to outbreaks, and 43 new outbreaks began during the year. The isolated case, in which one person picks up the virus and no one else gets sick, has almost vanished from the count. In a year driven by outbreaks, the danger is concentrated in the communities where a cluster has formed, while cases elsewhere remain rare.

Fewer new outbreaks began in 2026 than in 2025, when there were 48, yet this year's case count still climbed past last year's. Part of the explanation is that some outbreaks never ended. This year's count includes 1,385 cases from outbreaks that began in 2025, so a large share of the 2026 record was already underway before the year started. The record reflects outbreaks that kept going from last year as well as the ones that started this year. When the same chain of infection continues from one calendar year into the next, measles has become a lasting problem in those communities rather than a passing one.

Health officials in each state now hold the most current view of these outbreaks and run the response to them. The national count is a running total for the year, and the CDC itself says state health departments keep more current figures on the outbreaks within their borders. Pennsylvania is home to the country's biggest outbreak, according to Contagion Live. In New York, Gov. Kathy Hochul responded in October by declaring a disaster emergency for the state. Anyone living close to one of these clusters should check with their own state health department for the newest numbers.

Most people picture measles as a childhood illness that passes through classrooms and daycare rooms. In 2026, though, adults accounted for as much of it as school-age children did. Adults 20 and older and children ages 5 to 19 each made up 41% of confirmed cases through Nov. 5, according to preliminary figures from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, and children under 5 were the smallest group at 18%. Measles this year is not mainly a worry for parents of young children, and grown-ups make up as large a part of the spread as students do.

The large adult share came late in the year. Adults made up 29% of cases across all of 2025. Their share was 30% in July 2026, according to HealthDay. For the adult share of the full year to reach 41% by November, the cases recorded from summer onward must have involved adults more often than the earlier ones did. Any single age breakdown is a snapshot, and the groups that see new cases can keep changing as long as transmission continues. That shifting profile is a sign of a disease that has settled in, not an outbreak that comes and goes.

The wide range of ages among patients is evidence of a gap in protection that opened over many years, not in a single season. "That tells us this has been years of undervaccination in some communities," Dr. William Moss, a professor of epidemiology at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, told HealthDay. The people left unprotected over those years now include older children and adults, not just the newest group of kids.

In communities where vaccination rates have fallen, no age group is safe, and the question that matters is how well each community kept up its vaccinations.

This year's record measles count does not mean the vaccine has stopped working. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reports that 96% of 2026 cases were in people who were unvaccinated or whose vaccination status was unknown. Only 4% of patients were known to have been vaccinated, according to NBC 26. The disease is spreading among people who never got the shot or whose protection nobody can confirm, and it is rarely breaking through the vaccine.

People who finished the full two-dose course barely appear in this year's count, making up just 2% of 2026 cases. For a family deciding on the shot, completing both doses puts a child in the group that has stayed almost entirely out of this year's outbreaks.

The same lopsided split held through last year's outbreaks. In 2025, 93% of cases were unvaccinated or of unknown status. This year's share is higher still, so cases have stayed concentrated among the unprotected for two straight years, and measles has not moved into the vaccinated population in any meaningful way. Two years of the same pattern point to gaps in vaccination, not a weakness in the vaccine, as the reason measles keeps spreading.

The 2026 case count amounts to a tally of the people that vaccination has not reached.

Hospitals have treated far more measles patients this year, even though a person who catches the disease is no more likely to need a hospital than a year ago. About 11% of this year's cases needed hospital care, the same share as in 2025, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. The jump in hospital patients comes from the jump in cases, with 562 measles patients hospitalized so far this year, up from 244 in all of 2025. A steady rate can sound like reassurance. With an outbreak that keeps growing, the same risk still means more patients in hospital beds every month the spread continues.

For someone with no vaccine protection, the chance of a hospital stay is much higher than 11%. Dr. Dennis Wang, an Epidemic Intelligence Service officer at the CDC, and his colleagues report that approximately 20% of unvaccinated U.S. measles patients were hospitalized. The 11% figure counts everyone who caught the virus, including people with some protection and mild cases that may barely register. That is why the national figure can be lower than the risk facing one unprotected child. The unchanged national rate is not a sign that measles is getting milder. It reflects this year's mix of patients, not a fixed trait of the disease.

The patients who did end up in the hospital were often seriously ill. In the west Texas outbreak, most of the hospitalized patients whose records were reviewed needed extra oxygen, according to the CDC. None of those patients was admitted only to keep the virus from spreading to others. Each of those hospital stays reflected the patient's own illness.

The west Texas records cover only part of one outbreak, but there, at least, a hospital bed went to a patient who was seriously ill.

School-age children are the least likely to end up in the hospital with measles, and the odds rise sharply at both ends of the age range. This year 17% of infected children under 5 and 15% of infected adults were hospitalized, compared with 4% of school-age children, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. For parents, a preschooler with measles faces far higher odds of a hospital stay than a 10-year-old does, and grown-ups face odds nearly as high as toddlers. In west Texas, the most serious hospital stays involved children. The CDC reports that all four hospitalized patients admitted to intensive care in the early months of that outbreak were under 18. The number is small, but no hospitalized adult in that period needed that level of care.

The adult rate deserves close attention, because adults make up a growing share of patients the longer an outbreak lasts. By the end of the west Texas outbreak, adults accounted for 32.4% of all cases, according to the CDC. Because hospital stays are common among infected adults, a long outbreak moves many more people into an age group that often needs hospital care. If this year's outbreaks keep running, more adults may end up in the hospital, although that remains a possibility rather than a measured trend.

Some adult hospital stays may reflect caution rather than severe illness. The CDC reports that four of the five adults hospitalized in the early months of the west Texas outbreak were women in the third trimester of pregnancy, and that none of those women developed pneumonia or low oxygen levels. Part of the adult hospital rate may therefore come from precautionary care during pregnancy rather than from measles at its worst, though that evidence rests on a handful of patients in a single outbreak.

The very young are hospitalized most often and adults nearly as often, but in west Texas the patients who needed intensive care were all children.

More Americans have died of measles this year than in all of 2025, and the year still has weeks to run. The CDC counts four measles deaths in 2026 as of Nov. 5, compared with three for the whole of last year. Even before December arrives, 2026 is already the deadlier year. "This number is subject to change; CDC will update its reporting as additional information becomes available and relevant reviews are completed," the CDC said. Readers should treat four as the least that has happened so far rather than as a settled total.

The number of measles deaths this year depends on who does the counting. Pennsylvania announced a measles death on Sept. 30 that brought the nation's toll to five, HealthDay reports, while the national count at the time listed two because the CDC uses its own rule to decide which deaths to include. State announcements and the federal tally can therefore give different totals for the same year. Readers should expect the federal figure to sit below what states report.

The deaths have come from the places where outbreaks have grown largest. Pennsylvania, where the most recent death was announced, has the largest active outbreak in the U.S., according to HealthDay. While outbreaks of that size remain active, the toll remains open.

The number to watch is not whether the count rises, but where it rises next.

Fewer kindergartners are vaccinated against measles than the level needed to keep the disease from spreading. The CDC reports that 92.4% of children starting school in the 2025–2026 school year had their MMR shots, down from 95.2% in 2019–2020. Protecting a community from measles takes coverage above 95%, according to the CDC. In about six years, the country went from just above that line to below it. A shortfall of a few points matters with measles because the protective line is set so high, and even a small gap leaves enough unprotected children for one case to lead to others. The gap is large in human terms: approximately 280,000 children in kindergarten classes during 2025–2026 were at risk.

The national rate still understates the danger, because people catch measles from those around them, and vaccination levels differ from one community to the next. "At local levels, vaccine coverage rates may vary considerably, and pockets of unvaccinated people can exist in states with high vaccination coverage," the CDC said. The CDC lists lower kindergarten coverage and rising measles activity around the world as the reasons for the increase in cases. Lower coverage at home does not cause the cases that arrive from abroad. It determines whether one of those cases stays isolated or leads to an outbreak, depending on how many people near the patient are vaccinated. Large outbreaks can therefore happen despite only a modest national decline, wherever unvaccinated children are grouped in particular schools and towns.

For parents, the vaccination rate at their own child's school tells them more than the national figure does.

Measles did not drop out of American life on its own. Public health officials declared it eliminated because Americans vaccinated their children year after year until the virus could no longer circulate. The CDC reports that the vaccine was licensed in 1963 and that elimination was declared in 2000, after decades of vaccination. The CDC lists three conditions behind that result: an effective vaccine, high vaccination coverage among children and a strong system for responding to cases. The country earned the status by keeping enough children protected, not because the disease vanished from the world. Because elimination depends on that coverage, the country can lose it the same way it won it.

"Eliminated" sounds final, but the standard behind the word is narrow. A country keeps the label as long as measles has not spread continuously within its borders for a year or longer. A country can record imported cases and even outbreaks and still qualify, as long as each chain of spread ends within a year. A high case count alone does not decide the question. The answer depends on whether any chain of infection in the U.S. now runs unbroken for a year. That puts the weight on the outbreaks that carried over from 2025 rather than on the size of this year's total.

The weak point is how many people get the vaccine, not how well it works. Two MMR doses are about 97% effective, the CDC reports, and nearly everyone who receives both doses stays protected. Too few children are getting their shots across much of the country, and in 22 states fewer than 90% of children born in 2021 have received the MMR vaccine. Coverage was one of the three conditions behind elimination, and as it weakens, the year-long chains of infection that the standard rules out have more room to form.

Travelers $TRV will keep bringing measles in from abroad whatever the ruling, so the country's standing was always going to depend on how many people at home stay vaccinated.

Tesla and Cars &amp; Coffee Organizers Sued After Alleged 83-MPH Test Drive Ends in Head-On Crash

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A California woman claims a Tesla representative was showing off a Model Y's performance for photographers when he crossed into oncoming traffic and struck her SUV.

A 70-year-old California woman who reportedly suffered serious injuries in a head-on collision with a Tesla Model Y in the Bay Area this summer has filed a lawsuit against the automaker, the driver, and the organizers of a nearby Cars & Coffee event.

According to a civil complaint filed in San Mateo County Superior Court, a Tesla representative was demonstrating the vehicle's performance for event photographers. The driver allegedly reached speeds exceeding 80 miles per hour on a stretch of road with posted speed limits of 35–40 mph; the complaint claims he lost control on a blind curve, and crashed into Michelle Brooks's Honda CR-V.

The incident occurred on August 30, 2026, according to the filing, along State Route 84 near La Honda, California. The lawsuit identifies the Tesla driver as 21-year-old Micah Zuglian, who was allegedly driving a company-owned Model Y while attending a Cars & Coffee gathering at Ambassador Wine Garden. According to the complaint, Zuglian took two photographers associated with the event for a drive to demonstrate the Tesla's performance. Images allegedly captured during the drive show the vehicle's speedometer displaying 83 mph; another image reportedly shows a speed of 61 mph near a posted 35-mph speed limit sign.

The lawsuit alleges that Zuglian subsequently crossed the double-yellow lines on the road and struck Brooks's 2014 Honda CR-V head-on. Brooks had to be transported to Stanford Hospital for treatment of her injuries, which reportedly included fractured vertebrae, fractured ribs, broken limbs, and a traumatic brain injury.

The complaint alleges that representatives from the California Highway Patrol, San Mateo County Sheriff's Office, and a county supervisor's office met with event organizers beforehand and urged them to cancel the gathering over traffic safety concerns. The suit claims that roads in the area are notorious for racing, and that in the past five years, more than 20 people have died and almost 800 have been injured in automobile crashes in the vicinity.

Brooks alleges that Tesla negligently entrusted the Model Y to its representative and failed to adequately supervise him. The complaint also alleges negligence by the event organizers for proceeding despite the reported safety concerns. The plaintiff is seeking damages and requesting a jury trial.

The lawsuit also names Ethan Garabedian, doing business as “9burgring,” which organized the gathering, and Ambassador Wine Bars LLC, which hosted it. The allegations have not been proven in court, and the filing of a lawsuit does not establish liability on the part of Tesla, Zuglian, or the event organizers.

Probably Stolen Lou quest walkthrough: How to complete and all rewards

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The game’s most wanted criminal, no NPC in Probably Stolen can rival or challenge the mysterious (& dangerous) Lou.

And as you can expect, her cryptic persona persists in her quest, which can only be triggered and completed by playing a dangerous game. Here’s a full walkthrough on how to complete the Lou storyline in Probably Stolen.

Unlike most NPCs, like Serpico and Eleanor, Lou only appears on a random day after you purchase the Business Permit, available in the fifth week. During her visit, she will introduce herself before asking you for a series of ”tools” to be used on her next job. To trigger her quest and start her storyline, call Security on her and claim the 1.500 Credits bounty.

Now that you surprised Lou and made the questionable choice of turning in a specialist, a Republic Operative will pay you a visit in the next week, claiming Lou to be a traitor to the Republic and an alleged terrorist. He also gives you a Republic Transmitter and makes an offer: plant it into something and give it to Lou for 700 Credits, as she is sure to visit you again.

Sure enough, Lou will surprise you in a few weeks, inviting you to play a game and letting you know what’s at stake. The next morning, she reveals that a bomb is hidden within your wares and gives you until midnight to disarm it. Upon review, the Plastic Explosive will prove to be fake.

Read what’s behind the latest news in gaming (with a bit of snark) by subscribing to the Destructoid Checkpoint newsletter!

Following your fake brush with death, Lou will pay a visit to the store in around five days and leave her ”Box of Toys” in your care, before warning you not to touch it. The box features a document revealing more about her past and ties with the Republic.

She returns to grab it the next day. If you haven’t moved the contents of the box (you can still read the document at no risk) or placed the transmitter in it, she allows you to pick either 1x Advanced Turbo Booster, 1x Smuggler’s Bay, 1x Backpack (Small), or 2x Module Expansion Kits as a reward.

Following the encounter above, just keep playing until Lou visits under the guise of an inspector. She reveals that her ”guest’ has finally given her the info she needs, before asking for 750 Credits (or 500 depending on your chosen difficulty) worth of weapons, armor, or meds as a trade for the bounty you received earlier. You have one week to secure it. If you can’t, she asks for the amount in Credits on the due date.

We are now at the final stretch of her storyline, as Lou will thank you and vanish upon getting the agreed-upon ”tools” (or the necessary Credits) in her next visit. This will be your final meeting, with the news of the assassination of an admiral stamping the papers the next morning. She will send you a note, though, together with a Storage Bay Expansion Kit.

Lou’s quest/storyline then ends with another visit from the Republic Operative, who reveals they plan to pursue her and hints at your involvement in the whole ordeal.

With Lou’s quest done, don’t forget to also check out how to produce Huge Nutrifruits and unlock the Green Thumb achievement.

He joined the Destructoid team in May 2024 as a Contributing Writer and can always be found either thinking about Final Fantasy VII or eagerly waiting for news on Kingdom Hearts IV.

8 findings from the EPA on radon, the invisible gas in your home

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Eight findings from the EPA's radon page cover who bears the risk, how the toll estimates changed, and why a home test matters.

Radon can't be seen, so a family can live for years with the gas in an ordinary house and never know it is there. Researchers agree that radon gives people lung cancer, according to the US EPA. The uncertainty lies in the size of the toll. The yearly death figure that health agencies cite is a best estimate drawn from a model, not a count of people who died. The open questions are how many people radon kills and which people. A household can't wait for those answers before finding out whether it is exposed.

Protection against radon has to come before the disease, because lung cancer is hard to catch in time. From 2019 to 2023, close to half of lung cancer cases had already reached distant organs by the time doctors diagnosed them, according to the CDC. Many cases of the disease can be prevented, and keeping radon out of the home does more good than counting on doctors to find the cancer early. A household also can't rely on a neighbor's result, because radon levels in homes next door can differ very much, according to the NIH. Late diagnosis and a neighbor's reading both leave a family without an answer. A test of its own home is the practical line of defense.

The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency set out to show how radon in homes causes lung cancer and how serious that harm is. Each of those findings matters less on its own than in combination. A reader who weighs them together learns who carries radon's risk and how firm the numbers behind it are, and that is enough to decide whether to test.

Smoking is not the only major cause of lung cancer in the U.S., and the next one on the list is a gas inside ordinary homes. Only smoking causes more lung cancer than radon, and for Americans who have never smoked, radon causes more than anything else, according to the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency. About 21,000 Americans die each year of lung cancer caused by radon. Radon is a cause in its own right rather than a side note to smoking, and a family can live with it without knowing.

The CDC cites the same estimate of 21,000 deaths a year. That figure is a midpoint, and the EPA's full estimate runs from 8,000 to 45,000 deaths a year. The true toll could be well below the familiar number or well above it, and nobody can yet say which. Even the low end of that range means thousands of deaths a year. No household has a reason to put off learning its own level while the experts work out the national number.

Cigarette smoking has become uncommon among U.S. adults, only 12.5% of whom smoked in 2020, according to the CDC. Yet about 2,900 radon deaths a year occur among people who never smoked, and those deaths don't depend on anyone's cigarette use. Doctors who treat the disease see it changing. "This is not your grandfather's lung cancer," said Dr. Timothy Mullett, medical director of the Markey Cancer Center Affiliate Network at the University of Kentucky.

Those deaths among never-smokers can come down only through repairs to the house.

Lung cancer leaves few of its patients alive for long, and that is why radon's harm is counted in deaths rather than in illnesses people recover from. The EPA puts the share of patients who live five years past their diagnosis at 11 to 15 percent. Most lung cancer patients never reach a point where they have been treated and are well again.

That low survival, repeated across a large number of patients, produces a death toll that returns every year. In 2024, 128,663 Americans died of lung cancer, according to the CDC. With so few patients surviving five years, those deaths are the expected result of the disease rather than a handful of unlucky cases. Radon-related cases are part of that national toll, not a separate one, and with survival this low, each new case is more likely to end in death than in recovery.

Measured in deaths, radon is a bigger lung cancer threat than the secondhand smoke many people already go out of their way to avoid. The agency estimates that about 21,000 people die each year of lung cancer from radon, while the yearly toll it ties to secondhand smoke comes to an estimated 3,000. Radon gas in a basement deserves at least the caution people show toward a smoker in the room.

In a disease this hard to survive, a household gains the most by lowering its exposure before any cancer starts, and a radon test shows whether there is exposure to lower.

For a smoker, radon is not a separate hazard added to the cigarettes. The risks from the two are compounded, and the danger from each is greater when the other is present. The EPA estimates that about 62 in 1,000 smokers could develop lung cancer in homes with radon at 4 pCi/L. NIH reports that the EPA wants homes repaired once radon reaches 4 pCi/L or above, and that smokers account for most of the people who die of radon-related cancer. For anyone who smokes, the official line for action already marks serious risk rather than giving an early warning. In any household, the smoker has the most riding on the radon reading.

A reading under the action level is not a safe reading for a smoker. For readings from 2 to 4 pCi/L, the EPA's advice is that homeowners think about fixing the house, according to NIH. Yet at 2 pCi/L, the lowest reading in that band, about 32 smokers in 1,000 could develop lung cancer. Repairs also have a limit, because getting a home's radon level under 2 pCi/L is hard to do. A smoker whose home is fixed down to that level still lives with the risk that comes with it. Repairs can shrink radon's added danger for that smoker, but they can't erase it. For a smoker, that middle band is better treated as a reason to fix the house than as a reason to wait.

The odds of lung cancer are higher at each step up in radon, so a smoker needs to know exactly where the house sits, not roughly. A test that runs for months gives that answer better than a short one does. A long-term radon test lasts more than 90 days, according to NIH. That is long enough for a smoker to get a steady picture of the home's level before deciding whether to fix it, and how far.

A smoker can't undo the harm of past smoking, but with a careful test, a smoker can learn how much of the danger comes from the house itself.

Many people think of lung cancer as a smoker's disease and assume that a life without cigarettes keeps them clear of it. Radon in the house can still cause the disease in someone who has never lit a cigarette. The EPA estimates that about 7 in 1,000 lifelong nonsmokers could develop lung cancer in homes with radon at 4 pCi/L. Avoiding tobacco does not cancel out the risk from the air in the house. Because that risk comes from the home rather than from a habit, quitting or avoiding anything won't lower it, and the way to reduce it begins with a test of the house.

A never-smoker's odds depend on how much radon is in the air at home. For lifelong nonsmokers in a house at 20 pCi/L, about 36 in 1,000 could get the disease, a far more serious risk than at the lower level. Next-door neighbors with no smoking history may carry very different odds, and the only thing separating them is the radon in each home. For them, the useful question is not whether they are exposed but how much, and only a test of the home can answer it. Nothing about a person shows whether they live with a small risk or a serious one.

Experts disagree over where radon ranks among the causes of lung cancer in lifelong nonsmokers. In the EPA's estimates, radon is the leading cause among non-smokers. The researchers' estimates that the CDC cites put secondhand smoke ahead, with about 7,300 lung cancers a year in never-smokers linked to secondhand smoke and about 2,900 linked to radon. Both sets of estimates count radon as a real cause of the disease in this group, and they differ only on whether it outranks secondhand smoke. Even with those figures putting secondhand smoke ahead, the CDC advises never-smokers to test their homes for radon. Either ranking leads a household to the same step.

For someone who never smoked, the reading in their own house matters more than radon's rank on any national chart.

In 2005, researchers moved the proof of radon's harm out of the mine shaft and into the house. Until then, the case against radon in homes rested on people who worked underground, so its effect in kitchens and bedrooms was an inference drawn from miners. Researchers who studied homes directly had produced mixed results, with some finding a link, some a weak one and some none at all, according to the University of Iowa. The EPA reports that two 2005 studies, one in North America and one in Europe, supplied the direct evidence from homes that earlier work lacked. R. William Field, an associate professor of occupational and environmental health and epidemiology at the University of Iowa, says the European researchers reached the same answer as his North American team, which pooled data from seven home studies. That answer matched the risks the miner studies had predicted, so the danger in houses was no longer a projection.

The 2005 results mattered to homeowners because the risk showed up in ordinary houses rather than only the worst ones. In the North American analysis, prolonged exposure to radon at an average of about 3.0 pCi/L, a level below the EPA's action level, was linked to an 11 to 21 percent rise in lung cancer risk, according to the University of Iowa. That added risk turned up in houses whose owners could have been told no repair was needed. The finding supports the view of Tom Kelly, former director of the EPA's Indoor Environments Division, that even low radon levels can cause the disease. Field says an earlier study of homes in Iowa suggests the true risk from years of breathing radon at home may be slightly higher than the pooled range shows.

A homeowner should read a radon test as a number rather than as a pass or a fail.

By the end of the 1990s, the evidence on radon in homes needed one authoritative account that could support a national death estimate. The National Academy of Sciences published that account in 1999 in its BEIR VI report, which the US EPA calls the most definitive scientific collection on indoor radon. Later work built directly on it. The EPA's later range of radon deaths was the agency's own calculation, and it started from the methods BEIR VI laid out. The newer federal figure therefore grows out of this report's work rather than setting it aside.

The report is usually summed up as a single number, and that number makes it sound more certain than it was. The figure most often tied to BEIR VI is about 15,000 radon deaths a year, yet the US EPA reports that the report's estimate ran from about 15,000 to 22,000. That makes 15,000 the bottom of the report's range, not its final word. The range expresses doubt about how many people radon kills, not about whether it kills them. A later figure above 15,000 does not break with BEIR VI on that fact alone.

That range starts at a yearly toll already in the thousands. Any shift within it leads to more deaths, not fewer. A family waiting for scientists to agree on one number would be waiting on a question about how large the danger is, not whether it exists.

Only a test of the family's own home shows whether its air adds to that toll.

Refining the science on radon made its toll look worse. The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency reports that in 2003 it combined the National Academy of Sciences' BEIR VI findings into one model and raised its best estimate of radon deaths to about 21,000 a year. The agency consulted its Science Advisory Board on the revision, so the higher number reflects a scientific review and not only one office's calculation. When the EPA refined the estimate, it ended up with a larger toll. That leaves less room for the hope that earlier fears about radon were overblown.

The 21,000 figure is a best estimate inside a wide range, not the worst case. By the EPA's reckoning, the yearly toll could be as low as 8,000 deaths or as high as 45,000. Even the low end of that range means thousands of Americans dying of lung cancer every year, and no reading of the uncertainty makes radon a minor risk. The doubt in the estimate gives a household a reason to treat the familiar figure as a best guess and no reason to relax.

Federal health agencies give the public the number the EPA calculated in 2003. The CDC repeats the EPA's estimate of yearly U.S. deaths as the national toll on its radon page, which it updated Aug. 26, 2026. The EPA also said it would revise A Citizen's Guide to Radon, the handbook it writes for homeowners, to reflect its new reckoning of the risk. That revision carries the updated science to people deciding whether to test their homes rather than leaving it in a technical report.

The estimate can't show whether any one house adds to that toll. Only a test of that house's air can answer that.

Every official warning about radon ends in the same instruction to households: test your home. The U.S. Surgeon General's warning sets the point for action at 4 pCi/L (150 Bq/m3) or more, according to the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency. That number tells a family when to fix the house, not when the air is safe. "There is no known safe level of radon," the CDC said. A reading under 4 means only that the home sits below the level at which the government urges repairs.

The concern reaches far past American homes. As many as 15% of the world's lung cancers may come from radon, the World Health Organization estimated in 2009. That estimate is an upper limit, but even as a limit it makes radon a global health problem rather than a local worry. In many countries, radon in homes accounts for 50% of the natural radiation people are exposed to. The house is a major source of that exposure, and it is also the place where people can find and fix the problem. U.S. and international health officials give the same advice: measure the air where you live, because no reading taken anywhere else can stand in for it.

Radon stays in homes through neglect rather than difficulty. Dr. Michael Repacholi, coordinator of WHO's Radiation and Environmental Health Unit, says the world has paid little attention to a risk that people can lower without much trouble. The fixes are ordinary home repairs, such as sealing cracks and covering the bare earth floors of crawl spaces, according to Department of Housing and Urban Development advice reported by the CDC. The CDC also recommends testing again after repairs, so a household measures whether the work lowered its level instead of assuming it did.

The national and international warnings differ in reach but send a family through the same steps: measure the air, repair the house if the reading calls for it, and measure again.

13 foods tied to less inflammation, and what the evidence actually shows

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Thirteen foods linked to lower inflammation, from berries to cherries, sorted by what research measured in people and what it didn't.

Inflammation is supposed to end. The body sends immune cells to an injury or an infection, they do their work, and the swelling goes down. Long-term inflammation is different, because it lingers instead of ending. That is why food is such an appealing answer to it. Groceries are cheap, people buy them every week, and no one needs a prescription for a carton of berries, so it is easy to believe that prevention can come home with the shopping. Many foods owe their reputation to a molecule inside them rather than to the food itself. How much a food can do depends on where researchers found that molecule's effect and at what dose.

The strongest results in people often come from doses that no ordinary serving supplies. The trials behind turmeric tested curcumin plus piperine supplements rather than the spice in a curry, according to Frontiers in Nutrition. Behind other foods, the newest research hasn't reached people at all. Swinburne University of Technology says its researchers are looking for money to test broccoli's key compound in people, a step the work has not yet taken. USDA ARS reports that intervention studies are still needed for its mushroom findings as well. A promising result and a proven one are not the same thing. None of this makes the foods useless. A bowl of mushrooms or a head of broccoli supplies a smaller and less certain version of what researchers measured in a lab or a capsule. The sensible approach is to treat these foods as a pattern rather than a prescription: eat them often and together, and don't expect any one of them to work like a drug.

Healthline set out to find foods that ease long-term inflammation in the body, a search that took in turmeric, dark chocolate, mushrooms and cherries, among others. These foods don't stand on equal footing. Some have a measured drop in an inflammation marker in people who ate or took them, while others have only a believable explanation of how they might work. Several foods also depend on company, because the body absorbs them better or they work better alongside a partner food, so how people eat them matters as much as whether they eat them at all. The useful test for each food is how strong its proof is and which meal it belongs in.

Strawberries have a result measured in people who ate them, not just a molecule that looks promising in theory. Strawberry eaters in a 2016 study, all of them adults carrying excess weight, ended up with less of the inflammation markers connected to heart disease, according to Healthline. A finding tied to eating a fruit is easier to act on than one tied to an extract, because it involves food a shopper buys, rinses and eats. The people in that study shared a health profile, though, so the result may not hold the same way for a lean, healthy reader. It shows a link between eating strawberries and lower markers, and that link is a reason to keep berries in the weekly rotation. It does not make them a treatment for heart disease.

Berries are usually treated as one family of fruit, but only one member of it has a measured result behind it. Blueberries, raspberries, blackberries and strawberries all carry a group of antioxidants called anthocyanins, and that compound is why berries count as anti-inflammatory food. It is also the whole reason blueberries, raspberries and blackberries are included, since no measured drop in inflammation markers is reported for any of the three. A bowl of blueberries has the same kind of compound as a bowl of strawberries without the same proof in people. For a shopper, the sensible reading is that strawberries have the evidence and the other three have the promise. A mix of berries is a good habit for anyone eating to ease inflammation, as long as they know that the measured result belongs to strawberries alone.

Fatty fish is on the list because of a specific claim that names two fats and one measurable sign of inflammation. Salmon and other oily fish are rich in the omega-3 fats EPA and DHA, which lower CRP, a blood marker of inflammation, according to Healthline. That gives a reader something concrete to weigh, rather than a loose promise that fish is good for you. The important word is "fatty." The benefit comes with the fat, so the oily fish are the ones that count, and seafood in general does not offer the same thing.

Part of the benefit appears only after the meal, when EPA and DHA are converted inside the eater's body into resolvins and protectins. A person who eats the fish takes in the raw ingredients, and that conversion completes the work. The fats are part of the fish itself, with no capsule involved. That makes fish a dinner choice rather than a medicine, and it suits a way of eating that people repeat week after week.

Salmon is the obvious choice, but it is only one of several good sources. Herring, sardines, anchovies, and mackerel are all rich in EPA and DHA as well, which lets a cook switch fish from one meal to the next. That range makes the advice easier to keep up over many months than a single "superfood" purchase would be. A tin of sardines at lunch and a salmon fillet at dinner both qualify.

Put oily fish on the menu often, and choose whichever kind you enjoy most.

Broccoli is on this list because of one compound that targets inflammation at its source, not because of anything measured in people who ate the vegetable. Its sulforaphane suppresses inflammatory cytokines and NF-κB signaling, according to Harvard Health Publishing. Those are the chemical messengers and the signaling inside cells that keep inflammation going, so the claim is that sulforaphane turns down the process itself, and that is why broccoli sounds so promising. That account explains how broccoli might help, but it says nothing about how much it would help. No serving size, dose or drop in an inflammation marker comes with it, so a reader who wants to eat broccoli for inflammation has no portion to aim for. Broccoli's case rests on a believable process, with no measured result in people attached to it.

The newest serious research on sulforaphane is aimed at a rare inherited nerve disease, not at everyday inflammation. Swinburne University of Technology reports that sulforaphane increased levels of frataxin, a protein, in work on Friedreich ataxia led by Associate Professor Faith Kwa. Approximately 200 Australians live with that disease, and the work serves that small group of patients rather than shoppers deciding what to cook. The research asks whether the compound can raise frataxin, not whether broccoli calms inflammation, so its results can't confirm the vegetable's reputation on this list.

Scientists' interest in sulforaphane is a fair reason to keep eating broccoli, but it signals potential, not a promise about anyone's inflammation.

The case for avocados rests on people who ate the fruit itself, at the table, rather than on an extract of one of its compounds. Researchers recorded lower IL-1β and CRP, two markers of inflammation, after 51 adults spent 12 weeks eating avocado in a 2019 study, according to Healthline. That is a measured result in people from an ordinary food that anyone can buy and slice. The study was small, though. It is a real signal in humans, but it says less about the wider public than a larger trial would. Its length also matters for anyone planning meals around it, because the participants kept eating avocado for the full 12 weeks. Whatever avocados do for inflammation, they do it through steady eating over time, not through one serving or one salad.

Avocados also contain more than one family of compounds that interest researchers, so their appeal does not depend on a single molecule. A lower risk of heart disease has been tied to two of those families, the fruit's tocopherols and carotenoids. That link is an association, which makes it a reason for hope rather than a proven effect. It is also softer evidence than the drop in inflammation markers among the adults in the 2019 study, and that trial remains the stronger reason to put avocados in the shopping cart. A reader who eats them for their heart is acting on promise, while a reader who eats them for inflammation has a result in people behind the choice.

Keep avocados on the plate through the week as part of a way of eating, and don't count on them as heart medicine.

Green tea is on the list because of one molecule in it, a compound called EGCG, rather than because of the drink as a whole. Harvard Health reports that EGCG limits the output of inflammatory cytokines, the proteins that drive inflammation, and shields the fats in cells from damage. That gives the compound a role at two stages, curbing the signals that start inflammation and reducing the harm it causes once it begins. Both effects play out inside cells, though. They explain how green tea could help without showing that it helps anyone who drinks it, which places green tea with the foods whose case rests on a mechanism alone.

Green tea's healthy image comes from a different kind of evidence than the inflammation claim needs. The drink's good name rests on its links to lower disease risk rather than on a measured drop in an inflammation marker. That leaves open whether what EGCG does in a cell carries over to what a daily cup does in a person's body. A reader who drinks green tea for its general reputation should keep that reputation apart from the narrower claim that it eases inflammation.

None of this is a reason to stop drinking green tea. It is cheap and easy to make, and it fits the pattern of eating this slideshow describes, in which many good foods eaten often matter more than any single one. Drink it because you enjoy it and because it belongs in a sound diet.

Just don't brew a pot expecting it to lower your inflammation.

Peppers make the list for a group of everyday nutrients rather than one famous molecule. Healthline reports that the vitamin C, quercetin, and sinapic and ferulic acids in peppers may help calm inflammation. Those are common nutrients, and ordinary peppers in any produce section contain them. The kind of pepper a shopper picks still changes what ends up in the cart. Sweet bell peppers supply the quercetin, and both bell peppers and chilies provide vitamin C. Only chilies carry capsaicin, the compound that makes a pepper hot, according to Tokyo University of Science. A shopper who treats peppers as one food misses that the heat may be the more interesting part of the story.

Chilies have the stronger case in the lab, because capsaicin had the strongest effect of any compound tested alone, according to Tokyo University of Science. Even so, researchers saw the largest effect only when they paired capsaicin with other compounds. "When capsaicin and menthol or 1,8-cineole were used together, their anti-inflammatory effect increased several hundred-fold compared to when each compound was used alone," said Gen-ichiro Arimura, a professor at Tokyo University of Science. That increase describes a mixture of purified compounds, not a chili on a plate.

Nobody has measured the effect of the nutrients or of capsaicin in people who eat peppers. No human trial or inflammation marker supports the nutrient claim. The capsaicin result came from mouse immune cells in a lab dish, according to Tokyo University of Science. Peppers therefore belong with the foods backed by a plausible mechanism. They are worth eating, but they are not a proven remedy.

Eat peppers, sweet or hot, for the vitamin C they supply and in well-seasoned dishes, and treat the capsaicin finding as a reason for curiosity, not a dose to chase.

Mushrooms are on this list for the compounds they contain, not for any effect measured in a person's body. Healthline says the phenols and antioxidants in mushrooms give anti-inflammatory protection. That claim names compounds, and no dose or drop in an inflammation marker comes with it. The closest test of mushrooms against inflammation was a cell culture experiment focused on brain inflammation, according to USDA ARS. That leaves mushrooms with a plausible mechanism and nothing more. They are also very low in calories, so a cook who adds them to meals takes on little risk even if the benefit stays uncertain. That makes them an easy, low-risk part of a way of eating, not a treatment.

The word "mushroom" covers thousands of varieties around the world, and the lab evidence covers only one of them. The researchers tested a single species, the king oyster, Pleurotus eryngii, USDA ARS reports, and their results can't be assumed to apply to all edible mushrooms. Commercially grown mushrooms such as truffles, portobello and shiitake were not part of that work. A cook who grills a portobello has no test result for that mushroom to rely on.

The lab work did find real anti-inflammatory activity in the king oyster. Nitrite production, one marker of inflammation, was significantly lower in cells treated with every extract and compound the researchers tested, according to USDA ARS. Researchers at Universite Grenoble Alpes got their strongest results from an acetone extract and an ethanol extract, both rich in N-acetyltryptamine, or NAT, and from pure NAT. Ergothioneine, another compound they tested, was not among the best performers. None of these results has yet been tested in people who eat king oysters.

Grill or sauté whichever mushrooms you like, and count the king oyster findings as early lab work rather than a reason to eat more of them.

Grapes are prized for two compounds inside them, anthocyanins and resveratrol, rather than for the fruit as a whole. Researchers can therefore study the benefit, and companies can sell it, without anyone eating a grape. The human evidence for grapes comes from people who were already seriously ill. In a 2020 study, 60 heart-failure patients showed lower IL-6, a marker of inflammation, according to Healthline. A result in heart patients tells a healthy reader little about what grapes would do for them. The patients also took resveratrol rather than eating the fruit, so the finding belongs to one compound taken at a steady dose. Any real benefit from grapes depends on the compounds, so the question that matters at the table is how much of them a person needs.

Grape compounds lowered inflammation in people only when people took them daily for months, the way a patient takes a treatment. In one high-quality systematic review, CRP, another inflammation marker, dropped only for people who took more than 500 mg of grape polyphenols daily and kept it up for 12 weeks or longer, according to News-Medical. The heart-failure patients likewise took their resveratrol every day for 3 months. A person who snacks on grapes now and then gets a smaller and less regular amount of these compounds, so neither result carries over to a fruit bowl. Even at those doses, the improvement was a number in the blood, and a better blood reading is not the same as fewer heart attacks or strokes.

Eat grapes as a sweet, easy part of a diet full of fruit, and don't expect a handful to do what months of daily doses did.

Turmeric earns its place with a result measured in people, not a process seen in a lab dish. In a 2015 trial, people who took 1 gram of curcumin, turmeric's active compound, with piperine every day had lower levels of CRP, a blood marker of inflammation, according to Healthline. Researchers compared them with a group given a placebo, Frontiers in Nutrition reports, and everyone in the trial had metabolic syndrome. The drop showed up in patients with a diagnosed condition, not in healthy people eating a spiced meal. That makes turmeric one of the few foods here with a result in people, though the result belongs to a pairing.

Black pepper supplies much of what makes curcumin count, and piperine raises the body's absorption of curcumin by 2,000%. Piperine is a compound in black pepper that slows the rate at which the body clears curcumin and helps the gut take more of it in, according to a review of trials in Frontiers in Nutrition. Much of the trial's success therefore depends on an ingredient that isn't turmeric at all. Researchers tested the two together, and no one has yet worked out how much of the benefit comes from each. Anyone who cooks with turmeric should put black pepper in the same pot. That is the closest kitchen version of the tested pairing, but a pinch in a curry is a sensible habit, not a measured dose.

Curcumin's record in people is real but uneven, and it is more modest than the label "strong anti-inflammatory" suggests. Most of the trials in the review lowered CRP or IL-6, but some did not, according to Frontiers in Nutrition. The size of the drop also varied, with a significant CRP decrease in the 2015 trial and only a marginal one in a 2024 trial. The 2015 result sits at the strong end of that range. No trial ran longer than 12 weeks, so nobody yet knows whether the drop holds over the long stretch that chronic inflammation lasts.

Turmeric's promise depends on its partner and its dose, so use it as a seasoning alongside black pepper, not as a treatment.

Extra virgin olive oil earns its place on two counts: an antioxidant it contains and its role in Mediterranean cooking. Healthline reports that the effect of oleocanthal, an antioxidant in the oil, has been compared to that of ibuprofen, and that research suggests a Mediterranean diet combined with the oil may significantly lower inflammatory markers. The ibuprofen comparison applies to oleocanthal's effect, not to the oil as a whole. A drizzle over a salad is therefore not a dose of a painkiller. The comparison describes how one compound behaves, and it does not mean a bottle of oil can do a pill's job.

Any drop in inflammatory markers is tied to the oil and the diet together, not to the oil by itself. The oil is a staple of Mediterranean cooking rather than an occasional extra, so its possible benefit is hard to separate from the meals it usually appears in. People who hope for that benefit would look for it in using the oil across many meals as part of how they eat. A spoonful taken by itself each morning is not the habit the research points to.

What a shopper fully controls is the bottle. People get less anti-inflammatory benefit from refined olive oils than from extra virgin, so a cheaper refined oil gives up part of what makes olive oil worth buying for inflammation. The words "extra virgin" on the label are the part of the name that carries the claim.

Buy the extra virgin bottle and cook it into Mediterranean-style meals, and treat it as an everyday habit rather than a cure.

Cocoa earns its place with a change researchers measured in people's arteries within hours of a dose of its flavanols. In a 2022 study, blood vessels worked better 3 to 8 hours after people took those compounds, which are credited with arterial health, according to Healthline. That timing sets cocoa apart from foods whose case rests on a process seen in cells alone, because the change showed up on the same day as the dose. The improvement came in how well the arteries did their work.

The result came from a deliberate, repeated dose rather than casual nibbling. The study's participants had their 852 mg flavanol dose twice each day, a schedule that turns a treat into a routine. Whatever their arteries gained came with keeping up that intake, not with an occasional square after dinner. The figure also counts flavanols, the active ingredient, rather than chocolate. No shopper can read that dose off a wrapper.

The usual advice for choosing a bar is to pick chocolate with at least 70% cocoa, which means more cocoa and less of everything else in the bar. That rule is buying advice and does not come from the 2022 study. One number counts cocoa content and the other counts flavanols, and nothing on a label connects the two.

Buy the darker bar because it holds more cocoa and less candy, but don't expect a few squares to match what people in the study got from their twice-daily dose.

Tomatoes are on this list because of a single compound, and that compound comes with a condition about how they are cooked. Lycopene may help lower pro-inflammatory compounds linked to several types of cancer, Healthline reports, and the body takes in more of it when tomatoes are cooked in olive oil. The possible benefit and the cooking instruction arrive together. How a cook prepares tomatoes therefore matters as much as whether they end up on the plate. That condition follows from the kind of compound lycopene is, a carotenoid.

The body absorbs carotenoids better when they are eaten with fat, and that is why olive oil goes in the pan with tomatoes. A cook who wants the benefit can simmer tomatoes in olive oil, an easy step in a sauce or a stew. That turns the tomato into part of a two-food habit, and it shows why these foods work better as a way of eating than as a set of separate remedies.

Even when they are cooked well, tomatoes support a narrower claim than readers might expect. The compounds that lycopene may help lower are the ones tied to cancers. The case for tomatoes cites no measured drop in CRP or any other inflammation marker in people who ate them. Tomatoes therefore have a plausible mechanism and a sensible cooking tip, not a proven result. They belong in the meal as a regular ingredient, not as a fix to check against a lab number.

Cook tomatoes in olive oil whenever you can, and count them as one good habit among many rather than a remedy.

Cherries have an anti-inflammatory effect that researchers measured in people's blood, not only in cells in a dish. In a 2019 trial, 37 seniors who drank cherry juice ended up with lower levels of CRP, a marker of inflammation, according to Healthline. The fruit's anthocyanins and catechins are the compounds credited with that drop. A lower CRP reading is a change inside the body rather than a theory about how a compound might behave. The seniors drank the juice for 12 weeks, so anyone hoping for the same result would need a daily habit rather than an occasional glass. They also got their dose from a drink anyone could pour at breakfast instead of from a concentrated pill.

One small trial in older adults cannot settle the case for a whole fruit. A shorter 2021 study gave tart cherry juice to healthy younger adults and found no such effect. The two results describe different groups of people, so they don't cancel each other out. The lower CRP may hold only for older adults like the ones in the 2019 trial. Cherries pass the test of showing a measured effect in one group of people, but not the test of working for everyone. Shoppers who want to act on the research should look for tart cherries, the variety researchers have studied most.

Eat cherries as one regular part of an anti-inflammatory diet, not as a food to count on to lower inflammation by itself.

Disney's 'Star Wars' is coming to Cosm domes ahead of 50th anniversary as part of multiyear film deal

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

On Thursday, the immersive entertainment technology company announced a new studio partnership with The Walt Disney Co. that will bring the original "Star Wars" to its domes early next year as part of a multiyear, multifilm collaboration.

The financial terms of the deal were not disclosed.

Cosm's massive concave screens will drop film fans into a galaxy far, far away starting in March, just ahead of the Academy Award-winning film's 50th anniversary in May.

"We are thrilled to add the Disney partnership," Alexis Scalice, head of Cosm Studios, told CNBC. "We do look for those opportunities of these like anniversary and commemorative moments. We look for those opportunities to celebrate with the fans, but also find this mutually beneficial partnership between both parties."

This will be Cosm's sixth theatrical collaboration with Hollywood, having already partnered with Warner Bros. to bring "The Matrix," "Willy Wonka & the Chocolate Factory," "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone" and "It" to its locations. The company is set to launch immersive screenings of Universal's "Shrek" in December.

Each Cosm dome has stadium-style seating, with booths and tables set on each level. The dome's width fills much of the viewers' periphery, giving the sensation that they are part of the onscreen environment.

In this case, "Star Wars" will be displayed in a large rectangle in the center of the dome while the background is transformed into the iconic planets of Tatooine and Yavin IV that are featured in the film as well as the infamous Death Star. Audiences will also see elements that will make it appear like they're traveling at lightspeed and getting trapped in an intergalactic trash compactor.

Cosm is working directly with studios Lucasfilm and Industrial Light and Magic as well as creative studio MakeMake to create the visuals and the sound mixing for the unique presentation of the film.

"We look for those moments where sound, music ... listening to Darth Vader's breath for the first time ... you feel those moments differently in an 87-foot diameter dome and 12k resolution with the sound system we have," Scalice said. "And it's one of the things that with experiential cinema and our technology that we were so excited and honestly privileged that we can take these defining moments for fans and work closely with our partners that doesn't really change the authenticity of it, it just enhances it in a different manner."

The premium experience will also be paired with a themed menu. Cosm has done this with other titles, such as "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone," serving guests throughout the screening with house-made shepherd's pie, pretzel wands with cheese dip and a box of Bertie Bott's Every Flavour Beans.

Cosm is trying to establish itself as a destination for fandom — both for sports and for film. It has a wide variety of live event offerings, which it telecasts using proprietary cameras mounted in arenas that broadcast back to the dome, as well as its expanding library of movies.

"What I love is that we really are kind of building these Cosm communities built on fandom," Scalice said. "It's the No. 1 foundation for our business."

And Star Wars is a powerful fandom. The franchise remains one of the top-ranking pieces of intellectual property across film, television and retail annually, and Disney is gearing up for a massive celebration of its semicentennial including merchandise launches, theatrical re-releases, its biannual Star Wars Celebration convention and collaborations with other brands.

Correction: This story has been updated to correct the spelling of Alexis Scalice's name.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/bon-secours-mercy-health-acquire-majority-stake-surgery-partners" hreflang="en">Bon Secours Mercy Health bumps up offer for majority stake in Surgery Partners to $6B valuation</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Bon Secours Mercy Health bumps up offer for majority stake in Surgery Partners to $6B valuation" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Bon Secours Mercy Health bumped the price tag on its pending transaction to acquire a majority share in healthcare services company Surgery Partners in response to an unsolicited offer the short-stay surgical platform received from a third party, the organizations shared this week.

The transaction now values the acquiree at $6 billion, or $25 per share, up from the initial $5.7 billion and $23.50 per share (see that story below). Additionally, the parties agreed to an additional $1.61 per share in ticking fees if the deal doesn't close by March 1, 2027.

The companies' announcement does not name the third party, which offered $28 per share in a Nov. 3 proposal. A report from Bloomberg, citing an unnamed source, names UnitedHealth as the extra bidder whose offer was rejected over concerns about deal complexity and ability to close.

Surgery Partners’ business platform includes ambulatory surgery centers, surgical hospitals and about 5,000 affiliated physicians spanning 30 states. It reported $3.31 billion in revenue during 2025 and $0.47 adjusted earnings per share.

Affiliates of Bain Capital Private Equity currently control about 39% of Surgery Partners. Under the current deal, Bain would roll over its shares while the remainder would be acquired by a Bon Secours affiliate, HealthSpan Partners.

Bon Secours Mercy Health is acquiring a majority share in healthcare services company Surgery Partners in a take-private deal valuing the acquiree at $5.7 billion, the organizations announced Monday morning.

The transaction is expected to close in the first quarter of next year, pending approvals, and with over 200 locations in tow, would help the major nonprofit health system create “one of the country’s largest outpatient surgical care platforms,” they said.

“As healthcare continues to evolve, it is important to grow in ways that allow us to serve more people, in more communities and through a broader range of care settings,” John M. Starcher, Jr., president and CEO of Bon Secours Mercy Health, said in the announcement. “Our relationship with Surgery Partners represents an important step toward advancing that commitment."

Currently, affiliates of Bain Capital Private Equity control about 39% of the public Surgery Partners. Under the terms of the deal, Bain Capital would retain and roll over those shares, while HealthSpan Partners, an affiliate of Bon Secours, would purchase the remainder with cash at $23.50 per share.

Surgery Partners’ short-stay surgical platform includes ambulatory surgery centers, surgical hospitals and about 5,000 affiliated physicians spanning 30 states. It reported $3.31 billion in revenue during 2025 and $0.47 adjusted earnings per share.

The company has been public since 2015, with a 2017 merger making it the largest standalone, independent surgical facilities operator at the time. Bain Capital made an offer in January 2025 to fully acquire the shares it didn’t own for $25.75 apiece (roughly $6.7 billion enterprise valuation), though that offer was rejected a few months later.

Surgery Partners has also been slimming down some of its acute care holdings in recent months, a strategic move its leadership said would better position the company as a purely short-stay surgical provider while limiting its exposure to Medicaid.

“We believe this transaction represents the best path forward for Surgery Partners, delivering an attractive all-cash premium to our shareholders while creating lasting benefits for our patients, physicians and other stakeholders,” Surgery Partners CEO Eric Evans said Monday.

Bon Secours Mercy Health is a Catholic nonprofit with 47 hospitals, more than 1,200 care sites and nearly 60,000 employees. It reported just shy of $14 billion in total operating revenue through 2025, as well as $324 million of recurring operating income (2.3% recurring operating margin) and a $1.1 billion bottom line. Of note, its holdings include 41% ownership in revenue cycle management company Ensemble Health Partners, for which a summer strategic recapitalization transaction with a new private equity investor injected $670.8 million in cash into the system, per recent financial statements.

Bon Secours’ HealthSpan Partners is financing the Surgery Partners purchase with a combination of cash on hand and newly secured debt. Upon closing, the parties plan to keep Surgery Partners’ operations, brand and headquarters in place, while also keeping the business as a secular organization.

Strategically, the deal is evidence of large health systems’ growing investments in ambulatory care networks, where many expect to see rising demand as consumers prioritize lower-cost care settings. Healthcare executives have bluntly described these investments as a “top priority,” particularly as regulatory and policy activity such as the wind-down of Medicare’s inpatient-only list pushes care outside of the hospital.

“As more care moves beyond the hospital setting, patients are looking for high-quality care that fits more seamlessly into their lives,” Wael Haidar, M.D., Bon Secours Mercy Health’s president of clinical operations, said in the announcement. “Surgery Partners understands that care should be designed around the needs of patients, and that approach aligns closely with our mission. Together, we have an opportunity to expand access to high-quality outpatient care while supporting physicians and helping patients receive the right care, in the right setting, at the right time.”

The most apparent analogue for Bon Secours’ deal comes from Ascension, an even larger Catholic nonprofit, which closed a $3.9 billion deal over the summer to acquire AMSURG, an ambulatory surgery management services company with over 300 locations. Ascension CEO Eduardo Conrado has described that deal as both a means to flesh out his system’s existing markets—improving overall volumes and allowing hospitals to focus on high-complexity patients—and a way to strike new business partnerships with other local health systems in markets beyond its prior footprint.

Scout Motors has 212,000 vehicle reservations as $2 billion South Carolina plant nears completion

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

BLYTHEWOOD, S.C. — Electric vehicle startup Scout Motors has notched roughly 212,000 reservations for its upcoming EVs as the company nears completion of its new $2 billion plant in South Carolina, CEO Scott Keogh told CNBC.

The reservations are for the Volkswagen-backed company's Terra pickup truck and Traveler SUV, which are expected to start full production next year before deliveries begin to customers in 2028.

Reservations do not necessarily translate to sales, as the U.S. automotive industry has learned during the past decade, especially with highly anticipated all-electric trucks such as the Ford F-150 Lightning, Tesla Cybertruck and others that garnered strong interest that didn't lead to meaningful numbers of purchases.

Keogh said he expects Scout could roughly double those reservations by the time customer production begins, but he said the more important thing is to convert early interest into actual sales as production nears.

"It's been a consistent drumbeat," Keogh said during an interview at Scout's plant site outside Columbia, South Carolina. "I think we could easily be up over 400,000 with reservations without doing too much effort. The big thing for us is how much of the initial production can we 100% presell."

Keogh said achieving an order book of 12-to-18 months "would be great" and something he thinks the company can do before production. Scout on Thursday said construction is "in its final phase."

Scout's vehicles will be offered as all-electric versions as well as electric hybrid variants, known as extended-range electric vehicles, or EREVs, that include an engine as a backup generator for the electric motors and batteries.

Scout, a former American vehicle brand from 1961 to 1980, was expected to exclusively offer EVs in a bid for the German automaker to expand its presence in the U.S. However, slower-than-expected adoption of EVs and higher costs led the company to decide to offer EREVs as well.

Keogh said the EREVs and SUV are dominating reservations thus far, with roughly 87% choosing EREV models and 75% being SUVs.

The CEO also said the company is drawing interest from its target markets, which include owners of off-road vehicles such as the Ford Bronco and Jeep Wrangler as well as full-size pickup trucks and U.S. EV leader Tesla

Given the interest signaled by reservations, Keogh said the company is expected to launch with the EREVs first, followed by the EVs. He also said the company plans to launch with mid-level models first, a deviation from many companies that begin with their highest-priced models.

Both the Traveler and Terra are expected to start under $60,000. The EREV models are expected to feature more than 500 miles of range, according to the company, compared with 300 miles of range for the all-electric models.

"The No.1 thing for me is I want to go get the best volume as soon as possible," Keogh said. "In my mind, it's how quick can you get to 100,000 units? It's at 100,000 units where you can start to punch through."

His comments come months after Rivian Automotive received criticism online for launching its R2 SUV with its highest-priced models first, despite that being a common practice across the automotive industry.

Keogh said Scout's South Carolina plant is about 85% complete, but supporting infrastructure and a $300 million supplier park continue to be installed.

Reservations for Scout's vehicles initially opened in October 2024, when it revealed the Traveler and Terra, which feature sleek, yet rugged, exterior appearances that pay some homage to the brand's historic models.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: if a condo titled in a trust needs a replacement hvac will cost be considered as extra income to beneficiary that ye

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

@Celia Thanks for your helpful reply
Would the same reasoning apply to the purchase of a new vehicle to replace the old vehicle that was titled in the trust?
The trustee can usually provide the beneficiary with whatever in kind benefits they want, within reason, as long as the trust instrument or state law doesn't prohibit it. So if the trustee wants to purchase a car and let the beneficiary use it rent free, they can do that. (You might want that explicitly written into the trust instrument as something the trustee is allowed to do. Ask an estate planning lawyer for more information.)

As far as tax consequences, if the trustee distributes money to the beneficiary to buy a car, or gives the car outright to the beneficiary, then that's a distribution and it would carry out the trust's taxable income to the beneficiary.

If the trustee just let's the beneficiary use the car rent free, and the car remains in the trust, I don't think that is considered a distribution.

The trustee could pay for casualty (aka collision) insurance, major repairs, etc., and I think those would also not be distributions. But if the trustee pays usage related expenses like gas, liability insurance, etc., those might be considered distributions to the beneficiary.

Even if something is considered a distribution, note that distributions shift taxable income from the trust to the beneficiary, but don't usually create taxable income. Since trusts (usually) face higher tax rates than beneficiarys, distributions are (usually) a good thing when it comes to income tax. The downside is you expose the assets to beneficiary's creditors or estate tax liability.

So, yes, this can be done. For a moderately priced car that represents only a small % of the trust's total assets, it might be administratively simpler to just give the beneficiary the car, rather than trying to keep track of which expense falls into which category.

I suggest not trying to micromanage the future trustee's decision making. Tell the trustee what you want them to accomplish for the beneficiary, and and then give them discretion how best to do that. Maybe that means paying a stipend for the beneficiary's Uber rides rather than buying them a car, etc., etc.
