Film & TV Creatives Want More Climate Storytelling, But Face Barriers, Survey Finds

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: With the United Nations Climate Change Conference COP 31 underway, the results are in from a first-of-its-kind international survey of film and TV industry professionals about climate storytelling.

Deadline has seen the survey results, which reveal 71% of people surveyed want to include climate-related storytelling in their work. However, 55% of respondents reported barriers to including climate themes in their work, and there was also a lower level of overall enthusiasm for climate storytelling among commissioners.

Deadline was first to report the survey’s launch amid talk that film commissions and the UN itself were showing an interest in it, with the hope its results would inform the conversation and lead to more support for creatives working on climate-relevant projects. Related Stories Politics Hannah Einbinder Speaks Out After UN Arrest, Calls For U.S. To "Bring An End" To Israel Attacks On Gaza News Hannah Einbinder, Susan Sarandon Among Protesters Detained Outside UN Amid Netanyahu Arrival; Dozens Of Delegates Walk Out During Israeli PM's Speech

The survey was global and available in 15 languages, with responses received from 78 countries. The remit was to canvass the international industry, but with an extra focus on India, Bangladesh, Brazil, Nigeria, the UK and U.S. Watch on Deadline

The majority of survey respondents were either writers, directors or producers. 74% of respondents said they believed film and TV can help raise awareness of climate issues. Most respondents reported they would like to create solutions-focused content, or tell stories that weave climate conversations into everyday life.

With the results in, the report’s key recommendations included better funding and distribution for indie producers that incorporate climate themes in their work, and more efforts to ensure the studios, broadcasters, platforms and funders are properly engaged.

There was also a call for locally rooted and solutions-focused storytelling and support that is specifically tailored for different markets. The survey results suggest there are financial barriers that are notably hard to overcome in Africa and South America.

While climate change remains a charged issue, the overwhelming scientific consensus is that emissions, primarily from fossil fuel use, are driving global warming with wide-ranging consequences for people and the planet.

Futerra Solutions Union commissioned Ipsos to manage the survey on behalf of the Entertainment & Culture for Climate Action (ECCA) Film & Television Alliance. ECCA is convened by UN Climate Change to support the film & TV industry’s contributions to climate action.

Weil Gotshal partners vote to dissolve firm

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

According to Law.com, the next phase will center on paying creditors—chiefly landlords—and collecting outstanding receivables. (Photo from Shutterstock)

Partners at Weil, Gotshal & Manges have formally voted to dissolve the nearly century-old law firm.

Law.com reported Tuesday that 97% of the firm’s partners approved the move, which came about two weeks after the management committee recommended it Oct. 28. A three-person dissolution committee will oversee the firm’s dissolution, while Weil Gotshal executive chair Barry Wolf will act as a special consultant through the end of the year and longer if the wind-down runs into 2027. The firm will also utilize restructuring officers from management consulting agency AlixPartners and Davis Polk & Wardwell.

According to Law.com, the next phase will center on paying creditors—chiefly landlords—and collecting outstanding receivables.

Meanwhile, Law.com added that Weil Gotshal restructuring chair Matt Barr and several bankruptcy lawyers are in advanced talks to join Skadden, Arps, Slate, Meagher & Flom, which would remove one of Weil Gotshal’s last high-performing groups. Neither Barr nor Skadden responded to Law.com’s requests for comment.

Barr’s potential move would be the latest in a partner exodus that has helped bring down the once venerated Wall Street firm. According to Law.com, the firm saw nearly 50 partner exits in 2026, with sources estimating at least $500 million in lost business.
